- **Its frames.** A worker that a window started has `requestAnimationFrame()`, as Blink's `WorkerAnimationFrameProvider` runs it: frames come at the display's vsyncs, those of the Page's frames ([`native/page/worker_begin_frame_provider.h`](../native/page/worker_begin_frame_provider.h)), as tasks of the worker's compositor queue, each within half a period of its vsync on a display faster than Chrome's frames take to arrive, a late one is dropped, though not two in a row, so that a thread that wakes late still gets frames, and each frame runs all of its callbacks in one microtask, with the frame's time since the worker started, coarsened as `performance.now()` is. A worker that a worker started has no frames, and its `requestAnimationFrame()` throws Chrome's `NotSupportedError`.
- **Messages** go both ways on the serializer of `postMessage()`, ports and ArrayBuffers transferred among them: a worker's messages reach its `Worker` from a task of the creator's posted-message queue, and the `Worker`'s wait until the worker's top-level script has run, even if it threw, and then reach the worker from a task of its posted-message queue. Each `MessageEvent` has no origin and no source. A port's peer may be on another thread: its pipe is held under a lock, and a port reads from a task of its own thread. A `BroadcastChannel` reaches the channels of its name and origin in the Page's windows and workers and in the Session's other Pages.
- **Errors.** An error that the worker leaves uncaught fires an `ErrorEvent` at its global scope, whose `onerror` gets five arguments and cancels it by returning true. If nothing cancels it, a new `ErrorEvent` of the same message and place, with a null `error`, fires at the `Worker`, and if nothing cancels that, the same event fires at the creator's global object, which repeats this one level up for a worker that a worker started, and for a window the Session hears of it as an uncaught error. A promise rejected with no handler fires `unhandledrejection` at the worker's global scope alone.
- **Its end.** `close()` ends the worker once the task that called it ends: what waits in its queues never runs, and its `Worker` hears nothing more. `terminate()` ends it from the creator's side, drops the messages that wait for it, and stops a script that still runs 2 seconds later, as Blink's forced termination does. A worker also ends when its creator's context goes. The Session forgets a worker that ended (message 101).
- **Module workers.** `new Worker(url, {type: "module"})` runs the worker's script as the root of a module graph, as Blink's `WorkerModulatorImpl` runs it: the script comes through the Session as a classic worker's does, with the credentials of the options, and its module script is made from that response, which it needs a JavaScript MIME type for, with no request of its own, as `WorkerModuleScriptFetcher` hands the browser's over. What its graph imports comes from the worker's own module map, whose root is keyed by the URL that `new Worker()` asked for. Chrome fetches the top-level graph through the creator's settings, so each module of it goes through the Session as a request of the Kind `worker`, in mode `cors`, which the library sends from the creator: from the creator's origin, with the creator's site for cookies and, where the module's own is the empty string, its referrer policy, and with a module's lines and `Sec-Fetch-Dest: worker`, and its entry of the Performance Timeline, of the initiator type `script`, is the creator's, as Blink times what it fetches through the outside settings. A worker of a `data:` URL thus imports a script of its creator's origin that sends no CORS header, as in Chrome. The importing module's URL is the `Referer`, and a `data:` URL gives none. An `import()` goes as a window's modules do, from the worker itself, with `script`, and a worker's module sends `User-Agent` before `Origin`, as Chrome 157's worker does. A graph that fails, a module that does not parse or link, or a response that is not ok fires a plain `error` at the `Worker` and closes the worker; what the evaluation throws, or a rejection of its promise, goes the way of any error that the worker leaves uncaught. `import.meta.url` is a module's URL, and `importScripts()` throws Chrome's `TypeError`. Known Gap: a JSON module of the top-level graph goes from the worker, as a `module_json` request, where Chrome sends it from the creator, which only a worker of a `data:` URL shows, by its opaque origin.
- **`importScripts()`** fetches each script synchronously through the Session, as a request of the Kind `script`, of mode `no-cors`, at the highest priority and incremental, as Chrome's synchronous load of a raw resource is, after every URL has parsed, and runs it in order. A `data:` or `blob:` URL's script is read in the Page, with no check of its MIME type, as in Chrome. A script that does not load, or whose MIME type is not a JavaScript one, throws a `NetworkError`, and one that throws throws on, with Chrome's messages, but a script of another origin, whose errors are muted, throws a `NetworkError` in place of its own. `terminate()` stops a request that waits.
- **What it reads.** `location` gives the worker's URL, `navigator` what the creator's navigator gives of the Session's Persona and Machine, and `navigator.permissions` Chrome's answers for a context with no frame, which are `prompt` for background fetch and the storage access API. `Notification.permission` is `denied` there, `PerformanceObserver.supportedEntryTypes` a worker's, and `crypto` and `scheduler` work as a window's. `caches`, `navigator.locks` and `navigator.storage.estimate()` reach the caches, the Web Locks and the quota of its creator's storage key, which its creator shares ([The Cache API](#the-cache-api), [Web Locks and the Cookie Store](#web-locks-and-the-cookie-store)). `fetch()` goes through the Session with the worker as the context, which the library starts from the worker's script URL, in its creator's frame, with no client hints, as #265 recorded Chrome's, and with the referrer policy of its script's response where its own is the empty string, as an `importScripts()`, an `import()` and the script of a worker that it starts go; a body that is a stream goes as the Session reads it, as a window's does. `performance` counts from when the worker started.

Known Gaps: a worker of a `blob:null` URL, which a context of an opaque origin makes, and entries of `importScripts()` in the worker's timeline. A worker's Script Fingerprint is Chrome 157's ([parity.md](parity.md#the-script-fingerprint)).

## The bindings

What a page's scripts can reach of the Web's interfaces, and how, is part of its **Script Fingerprint**. So the Runtime's V8 bindings come from the Engine Release's Web IDL files, as Chrome's do, and are written by a generator of mitto's own, [`native/bindings/bindings_generator`](../native/bindings/bindings_generator), which GN runs on every build of the Runtime. Blink's own generator, `bind_gen`, writes code for Blink's classes, which mitto does not have. The generator follows `bind_gen` for everything a page can see: which properties each object has and in which order, their attributes, names and `length`s, and the errors that each call throws, with Chrome's messages. Each module names the files of Blink's that it follows on its `Upstream:` lines.

### How the generator works

It runs in four steps, all in [`native/bindings/generate_bindings.py`](../native/bindings/generate_bindings.py) and the package next to it:

1. **Read.** Blink's scripts build the [Web IDL database](building.md#the-web-idl-database) from the IDL files of every interface that Chrome exposes to pages, with their partial interfaces and mixins merged in. The generator loads it with Blink's own `web_idl` package and reads every definition into a model of its own ([`web_idl_reader.py`](../native/bindings/bindings_generator/web_idl_reader.py), [`model.py`](../native/bindings/bindings_generator/model.py)): interfaces, namespaces, dictionaries, callbacks, enumerations, and each of their members, types and extended attributes. It reads all of them, whether or not the Runtime implements them yet, and it refuses anything it does not know, with a message that names it and where it is: a kind of definition, a type, or an extended attribute that [`extended_attributes.py`](../native/bindings/bindings_generator/extended_attributes.py) does not list. That file gives each of the 73 extended attributes that the database of 157.0.8103.39 uses a role: what it changes for a page, or that only Blink's implementation reads it, such as `[Measure]`. So when an Engine Release brings a construct that nobody has looked at, the build stops with its name, instead of writing bindings that are wrong.
2. **Plan.** For every interface, [`install_plan.py`](../native/bindings/bindings_generator/install_plan.py) works out the own properties of its interface object, prototype and instances in the order that Chrome installs them. `bind_gen` installs the members that are always there on V8's templates first, then those behind a runtime-enabled feature, and later, on each context's objects, those that the context decides, such as `[SecureContext]` members. V8 adds `constructor` to the prototype between the two. Within each of these, Blink installs the attributes, then the constants, then the operations, which `web_idl` sorts by name, with the members of one condition together. `[LegacyUnforgeable]` members go on each instance, not configurable. Chrome's renderer takes the objects of `Window`, `Document`, `HTMLDocument`, `Node`, `EventTarget` and `WindowProperties` from its V8 context snapshot, which holds only the members that are always there, so for these the members behind a feature come last, on each context's objects. A worker's context comes from no snapshot, and of these only `EventTarget` is in one, with no member behind a feature.
3. **Write.** It writes into `out/runtime/gen/mitto/bindings` the bindings of every interface, namespace and callback interface that a Page's global objects expose, the window's and a dedicated worker's, and of each ancestor of one, in one of two forms:
   - For each interface that has a C++ class in the Runtime, [`cpp_writer.py`](../native/bindings/bindings_generator/cpp_writer.py) writes `v8_<interface>.h` and `.cc`: a callback for each attribute, operation and constructor, and the function that installs them on the interface's templates in the order of the plan. It converts values as Web IDL says, in the order of `bind_gen`'s callbacks: V8's signature check of the receiver, which throws "Illegal invocation"; then for a constructor that it is called with `new`; then the number of arguments; then each argument in turn ([`cpp_types.py`](../native/bindings/bindings_generator/cpp_types.py)). It handles only the types and extended attributes that those interfaces use, and refuses any other there, naming it: adding a class may mean teaching it more.
   - For every other, [`table_writer.py`](../native/bindings/bindings_generator/table_writer.py) writes a table: its `WrapperTypeInfo` and each of its own properties in the order of the plan, with its kind, attributes, `length` and condition. [`interface_table.cc`](../native/bindings/interface_table.cc) installs a table on the construct's template, and on each context's objects for what the context decides, as `bind_gen`'s install functions do. A table holds data and no code, so the thousand or so interfaces without a class cost the Page program under 1 MB and their build a few seconds ([building.md](building.md#times-and-sizes)). The tables go into a fixed number of files, `interfaces_without_class_<n>.cc`, which [`generated_files.gni`](../native/bindings/generated_files.gni) names, so that GN knows them before the generator runs.

   It also writes the dictionaries and unions that the classes' bindings use, the table of runtime-enabled features, and the functions that set up a window's global object and a dedicated worker's. The generator writes a file only when its content changes, so a change to the generator or to `interfaces.gni` compiles again only the files it changes.
4. **List.** It writes `web_idl_listing.txt`, every interface and member that it planned, one per line, with the features and conditions each is behind. The release routine compares it between two Engine Releases ([releasing.md](releasing.md#2-check-out-the-new-engine-release)).

A page sees every interface, namespace and callback interface that Chrome exposes on `Window`, as [ADR-0008](adr/0008-v8-and-bindings-from-web-idl.md) has it, behind the features and conditions of each: 1,061 in the listing of 157.0.8103.39. A dedicated worker's context sees those that Chrome exposes on `DedicatedWorkerGlobalScope`, whose `[Global]` names it `Worker` and `DedicatedWorker`. An isolate has either a window's contexts or a worker's, as Chrome's main thread and each worker's thread do, so a member that `[Exposed]` puts on one global object and not the other, such as `performance.timing`, is installed or not once for each isolate, on each context's objects, as `bind_gen` tests each context with `IsWindow()`. Those with a C++ class are in [`native/bindings/interfaces.gni`](../native/bindings/interfaces.gni), and every member of one without a class is a Known Gap. Each class is `mitto::<interface>` in [`native/dom`](../native/dom). A member whose behaviour the Runtime lacks is a **Known Gap**: its binding is installed as Chrome's is, so the shape matches, and [What a Known Gap does](#what-a-known-gap-does) says what happens when a page calls it. An entry there either lists its Known Gaps in `missing`, as `EventTarget` does for `when()`, whose `Observable` the Runtime does not have, or lists in `implemented` the members its class has, so that every other member is a Known Gap, as the DOM's large interfaces do. `get x` there names the getter of an attribute alone. The generator gives a reflected attribute, `[Reflect]`, its behaviour itself, through the element's content attribute, with the accessors of the element that `bind_gen`'s `_make_reflect_accessor_func_name()` picks: a string, `DOMString` or `USVString`, with `[LegacyNullToEmptyString]` or without, gives the content attribute or `""`; a nullable string, such as the ARIA attributes of `Element`, gives null for a missing one, which null removes; a boolean, whether it is there; a `long`, Blink's `GetIntegralAttribute()`, the HTML Standard's rules for parsing integers, and 0 for one that is missing or does not parse; an `unsigned long` the same, with 0 for a negative value, and sets 0 for a value above 2147483647, as `SetUnsignedIntegralAttribute()` does; and one with `[URL]` the content attribute without its leading and trailing HTML spaces, resolved against the document's base URL, or as it is if it does not resolve, and `""` for a missing one, as `GetURLAttribute()` does. One that `[ReflectOnly]` limits to its keywords, which may be nullable, such as `crossOrigin` or `fetchPriority`, gives the content attribute in lower case if it is one of them, and otherwise what `[ReflectMissing]`, `[ReflectEmpty]` or `[ReflectInvalid]` say, or null or `""`, as `bind_gen`'s `make_reflect_process_keyword_state()` writes it, and null removes the content attribute of a nullable one. The setter of a reflected boolean or `DOMString` of an element with `[CEReactions]` and nothing else that has a side effect throws "1 argument required, but only 0 present." when it is called with no argument, as the setter that `bind_gen`'s `optimize_element_cereactions_reflect()` shares does; every other setter converts the missing argument as `undefined`, as `bind_gen`'s leave out the check. A reflected `Element`, such as `popoverTargetElement`, stays with its class. The generator and leaves an indexed or named property operation that is a Known Gap out, so that a page reads nothing through it.

For Chromium 157.0.8103.39, the generator reads the whole database and writes everything in about 3 seconds, and the listing has 20,237 lines, about 1.2 MB.

### What a Known Gap does

A Known Gap does what the owner chose on 2026-11-12, in [#208](https://github.com/darionxt/mitto/issues/208), whose comment compares the options. Before, it threw `TypeError: Not implemented.`, which Chrome never says, so one caught error told a page that it did not run in Chrome, and the page's script stopped where Chrome's goes on. In short, it does what Chrome's binding does up to the call into Blink, tells the Session, and gives its type's default. Its callbacks are in [`known_gap.cc`](../native/bindings/known_gap.cc), the same for an interface with a class and one without, and they read the member's types and overloads from tables that the generator writes into `idl_types.cc` ([`type_tables.py`](../native/bindings/bindings_generator/type_tables.py)):

1. **Chrome's checks first.** In the order of `bind_gen`'s callbacks, with Chrome's messages, as the binding of a member with behaviour does, it:
   - checks the receiver;
   - for a constructor, checks that it is called with `new`;
   - checks the number of arguments;
   - resolves the overloads;
   - converts each argument as Web IDL says.

   For an operation or an attribute that returns a promise, an error from any of these rejects the promise, a wrong receiver among them: Chrome gives such a member no V8 signature check, and checks the receiver in the call instead. An operation whose IDL declares several overloads, even if the feature set has only one of them, goes through `bind_gen`'s overload dispatcher first, which checks the number of arguments and the distinguishing argument before the overload checks the receiver, so `CookieStore.prototype.set.call({})` rejects with "1 argument required, but only 0 present.". So a wrong argument fails as it does in Chrome, and the page's own getters, iterators, `valueOf()` and `toString()` run as they run in Chrome. The converted arguments are thrown away: only the call into the Runtime's C++ is missing. [`idl_conversions.cc`](../native/bindings/idl_conversions.cc) converts each type as Blink's `NativeValueTraits` do, with their messages, an array for a sequence by its indices after Blink's `RangeError` for more items than Blink's `Vector` of them holds, a union as `bind_gen`'s `union.py` tries its members, and a dictionary's members in order, with the member being read in front of an error. It resolves overloads as `bind_gen`'s overload dispatcher does: the items of Web IDL's effective overload set that take as many arguments as the call gives, and among them the one that the value of the distinguishing argument selects, by the same tests in the same order. A call that no overload takes throws "N arguments required, but only M present." with the fewest that any overload needs, or else "Overload resolution failed.".
2. **A report.** It tells the Session the first time a document reaches it after its arguments have converted, named as the listing names it, such as `Navigator.prototype.getBattery`, or `new Highlight` for a constructor. The bindings remember for each document which they reported, and the Page sends each report to the Session as a Known gap message, which reaches a caller as a `MITTO_PAGE_EVENT_KNOWN_GAP` event and Python as the Page's `known_gaps`. Chrome's use counters count a feature the same way, once for each document (`UseCounterImpl::Count()` in `third_party/blink/renderer/core/frame/use_counter_impl.cc`). A Known Gap inside a member's behaviour, such as a selector that needs more than simple selectors, reports the same way. The generator also marks each Known Gap in `web_idl_listing.txt`, so that each build writes all of them down, including those that no Parity Check sees.
3. **Its type's default.**

   | Member | What it does |
   | --- | --- |
   | Getter | Gives the value that its setter last stored on that object, or else its type's default |
   | Setter | Stores the converted value for the getter |
   | Operation | Returns its return type's default, a new object for an interface |
   | Operation that returns a promise | Returns a promise resolved with the default of the promised type |
   | Constructor | Returns an object of the interface with nothing behind it |

   The one exception, which the owner chose on 2026-11-12: a `toJSON()` that returns an object gives what Web IDL's default toJSON steps collect, so that a page sees Chrome's keys. It gives a new object with each attribute of a JSON type of the interface that declares it and of each ancestor that declares a `toJSON()` too, ancestors first, each read through the object as a page reads it. Enumerations count among the JSON types, as Blink's own `toJSON()` methods include them. So `JSON.stringify(new DOMRect())` gives Chrome's `{"x":0,"y":0,"width":0,"height":0,"top":0,"right":0,"bottom":0,"left":0}`. Blink has no default `toJSON()` of its own: each class writes one.

   A type's default is:
   - `null` for a nullable type;
   - `false`, 0 or `""`;
   - an empty array for a sequence or a frozen array;
   - `undefined` for `any`;
   - the first value of an enumeration;
   - for an interface, an object of that interface with nothing behind it, whose members are Known Gaps too.

   A getter gives the same such object each time on one receiver, unless the IDL says `[NewObject]`. An iterable, maplike or setlike declaration on an object with nothing behind it iterates over nothing: its `entries()`, `keys()` and `values()` give an iterator of Chrome's shape, whose prototype holds `next()` and the iterator's name for `@@toStringTag`, such as "URLSearchParams Iterator", and inherits from V8's `%IteratorPrototype%`, or its Map, Set or async iterator prototype, and whose `next()` is done at once, and each result of an iterator, a maplike's and a setlike's among them, has `done` before `value`, as Blink's `ESCreateIterResultObject()` makes it, where V8's own iterators, which a value iterable's members are, have `value` first. The generator writes the table of each such iterator itself, since Blink's database merges them into their interfaces. A pair iterable whose class has its behaviour, such as `Headers`', gets `entries()`, `keys()`, `values()`, `@@iterator` and `forEach()` from the generator, over an iteration source that the class makes for each, as Blink's `PairSyncIterable` has them ([`pair_iterable.h`](../native/bindings/pair_iterable.h)). Such an iterator is never finished: once a source has nothing left, its `next()` gives what the class gains after. A value async iterable declaration whose class has its behaviour, such as `ReadableStream`'s, gets `values()` and `@@asyncIterator` from the generator: the class makes the iterator's source from the converted arguments, and the iterator's `next()` and `return()` run Web IDL's steps with the ongoing promise that orders them, as Blink's `AsyncIterationSourceBase` does ([`async_iterable.h`](../native/bindings/async_iterable.h)).

Some members follow other rules:

- **Event handler attributes** are not Known Gaps. The generator gives them Chrome's behaviour, as it does for reflected attributes. The getter gives `null` until a handler is set. The setter stores any object, and turns anything else into `null` without throwing, as `JSEventHandler::CreateOrNull()` does. The handler is a listener of its EventTarget ([`event_handlers.h`](../native/dom/event_handlers.h)): it joins the listeners the first time it is set, keeps its place when it is set again, and goes when it is set to `null`, as the HTML Standard's event handlers do. A dispatch calls it with the current target for `this` and the event for its argument, does nothing for one that cannot be called, and cancels the event when it returns `false`, as Blink's `JSEventHandler::InvokeInternal()` does. For an `ErrorEvent` of type `error` at a window, `onerror` gets the HTML Standard's five arguments instead, the event's `message`, `filename`, `lineno`, `colno` and `error`, and returning `true` cancels it. An `HTMLBodyElement`'s and an `HTMLFrameSetElement`'s handlers of the window's events, such as `onload` and `onerror`, are its window's. An event handler content attribute, such as `onclick="..."`, sets the handler to its text, as Blink's `JSEventHandlerForContentAttribute` does ([`event_handler_attributes.h`](../native/dom/event_handler_attributes.h)): on an HTML, SVG or MathML element for each attribute of Blink's table in `HTMLElement`, and on a `body` or `frameset` element for its window's handlers. The text is compiled the first time the getter reads it or an event needs it, into a function named after the attribute, whose argument is `event`, `evt` on an SVG element, or the five of a window's `onerror`, with the element, its form owner and its document as scopes, from the document's URL and where its parser was, if it was parsing. Text that does not parse is reported as an uncaught error, once, and the handler is `null`, as it is in a document without a window. Removing the attribute removes the handler.
- **A stringifier attribute's `toString()`**, such as `URL`'s for `href`, gives what the attribute's getter gives, the value its setter stored among it, as Blink's `toString()` is that getter.
- **An attribute's setter** ignores a string that is not one of its enumeration's values, as Web IDL says, with Blink's warning in the console, such as `The provided value 'nonsense' is not a valid enum value of type ScrollRestoration.`, and one with `[PutForwards]` sets the attribute it names on what the getter gives, as `bind_gen`'s setters do. A setter of a nullable type, a dictionary, `any` or an event handler called with no argument throws "1 argument required, but only 0 present.".
- **An object of an interface without a class** whose nearest ancestor with a class is `EventTarget` or `Event` has an object of that class behind it, which the DOM makes ([`objects_behind.h`](../native/dom/objects_behind.h)), so that `addEventListener()` and `dispatchEvent()` work on a `FileReader`, and `type` and `bubbles` on a `CustomEvent`, which its constructor's `type` and `EventInit` give. Its own members stay Known Gaps.
- **An event's constructor** sets the attributes that its init dictionary names, given or by their defaults, as the DOM Standard's inner event creation steps do and each of Blink's event classes does: `new PointerEvent('pointerdown', {pressure: 0.5}).pressure` is 0.5, and its `width` 1, `PointerEventInit`'s default. Each such attribute's getter gives that value from then on, as what a setter stored. A sequence becomes the attribute's frozen array, and a dictionary an object of the page's with its members in order, as Blink's `ToV8` makes them. An integer member initializes an attribute of the same width and the other signedness with the same bits, as `KeyboardEvent` keeps `KeyboardEventInit`'s `unsigned long` `charCode` and `keyCode` for its `long` ones, so `new KeyboardEvent('keydown', {keyCode: 4294967295}).keyCode` is -1. Any other member whose type is not the attribute's, such as `TouchEventInit`'s sequence of `Touch` for `TouchEvent`'s `TouchList`, initializes nothing, and an attribute that Chrome works out instead, such as `MouseEvent.x` or `PointerEvent.altitudeAngle`, keeps its type's default.
- **An object of a class that a Known Gap may make**, one that [`interfaces.gni`](../native/bindings/interfaces.gni) marks `made_by_known_gaps`, is what the class's `CreateForKnownGap()` makes with nothing given: an empty `HTMLCollection` for `document.getElementsByTagName()`, a `DOMRect` of zeros for `getBoundingClientRect()`, an `EventCounts` with Chrome's 36 types for `performance.eventCounts`. An interface without a class whose nearest class is such a class gets one of its objects behind it the same way, as `HTMLFormControlsCollection` gets an empty `HTMLCollection`. So giving an interface a class takes nothing away from the Known Gaps that give its objects.
- **The generator refuses by name** a Known Gap whose default would be unsafe or impossible:
  - a member of `SubtleCrypto`, whose default would be a predictable digest or key;
  - an attribute or operation that gives an object of an interface that has a C++ class that a Known Gap may not make, whose class's members would find no object behind it.

  Each of these needs behaviour, or an outcome written by hand. Until it has one, it keeps `TypeError: Not implemented.` ([`known_gaps.py`](../native/bindings/bindings_generator/known_gaps.py)), and the listing marks it "Known Gap, refused". An object that a Page has no bindings for, such as one of an interface exposed only to workers, is refused the same way. For Chromium 157.0.8103.39, 76 members are refused.
- **The members that pages call most** get behaviour, or Chrome's outcome for the Page's state, written by hand, unless the default already is Chrome's outcome. These are the members used on at least 1% of page loads, by Chrome Platform Status's use counters. Chrome's outcome is the one for a Machine that has granted no permission, has only the devices it claims, and has no user activation. For example, `navigator.permissions.query()` gives `"prompt"`, where the first value of `PermissionState` would say `"granted"`. Where Chrome waits for a person, the outcome may be a promise that never settles, as `getUserMedia()` waits at its prompt. A member written this way is no longer a Known Gap. [The members pages call most](#the-members-pages-call-most) lists them, with what each gets.

The defaults keep a page's script running where Chrome's runs, and with it the requests the script makes. They are often Chrome's own values too. In a walk of a new page in Chrome 157, 85% of the getters within three steps of `window` gave their type's default, every event handler among them. A Parity Check therefore shows a Known Gap only where its default differs from Chrome's value.

#### The members pages call most

[#222](https://github.com/darionxt/mitto/issues/222) goes through the members exposed on `Window` that are used on at least 1% of page loads, by Chrome Platform Status's use counters of 2026-11-10, joined with the `[Measure]` and `[MeasureAs]` of the IDL of 157.0.8103.39 under the names that `bind_gen` counts them by (`_make_measure_web_feature_constant()` in `bind_gen/interface.py`). Only a member with one of those is counted, so `document.createElement()`, which has none, is not on the list. 457 members of 134 interfaces are. Of these, 71 already have their behaviour, from M6's DOM and from #214, #216, #217 and #218, such as `navigator.userAgent`, `screen.width` and `matchMedia()`; 43 get theirs from #215, which gives the window's and the document's values for a top-level page and the objects a page reaches without a call, such as `location.origin`, `history.length` and `document.scripts`; and 6 are behind a feature that is off for Windows, `NetworkInformation`'s `type`, `downlinkMax` and `ontypechange`, `window.orientation`, `defaultStatus` and `defaultstatus`. The other 337 get one of these, with the reason for each in the tables below:

- **Its behaviour**, where that is cheap and needs nothing from a later milestone.
- **Chrome's outcome** for a profile that has granted no permission, a Machine with only the devices it claims, and no user activation, from the Engine Release's source. Outcomes agree with each other: a feature's refusal and what `navigator.permissions.query()` says of it.
- **Its default**, because the default already is Chrome's outcome for the Page's state.
- **Its default until** the milestone of the roadmap that brings its behaviour, or because the roadmap leaves it out, or because no milestone has what it needs yet.
- **Nothing**, for a member of an interface whose objects a Page cannot have yet.

`atob()` and `btoa()` are on the list on the owner's word of 2026-11-13, not from the use counters, which do not count them: as Known Gaps they gave `""`, which broke base64, and the code that builds on it, without an error.

| Members | Page loads | Gets | Why |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `Crypto`: `getRandomValues()` | 48.9% | Its behaviour | It fills the view from the system's random numbers, as Chrome's renderer does, with Blink's checks: a view that is not of an integer type throws a `TypeMismatchError`, and one of more than 65,536 bytes a `QuotaExceededError`, an object of that interface. `randomUUID()`, which is not counted, gives a version 4 UUID the same way. `crypto.subtle` gives the window's `SubtleCrypto`, whose members stay refused below |
| `Window`: `atob()`, `btoa()` | Not counted | Its behaviour | The Infra Standard's forgiving-base64 decode and encode, as Blink's `Base64Decode()` and `Base64Encode()` run them on modp_b64, with Chrome's `InvalidCharacterError` for a string beyond Latin-1 or one that is not correctly encoded |
| `Performance`: `getEntries()`, `getEntriesByName()`, `getEntriesByType()`, `mark()`, `measure()`, `clearMarks()`, `clearMeasures()`; `PerformanceMark`: `new PerformanceMark()` | 67.7% | Its behaviour | The timeline of marks, measures, the navigation and each response that the Session gave the document, as [the Performance Timeline](#the-performance-timeline) says |
| `Performance`: `timing`, `navigation`; `PerformanceNavigationTiming`: `criticalCHRestart` | 58.2% | Its behaviour | The navigation's times, from the Session's load timing |
| `PerformanceResourceTiming`: `transferSize`, `encodedBodySize`, `decodedBodySize`, `deliveryType`, `initiatorType`, `workerRouterEvaluationStart`, `workerCacheLookupStart`, `workerMatchedSourceType`, `workerFinalSourceType` | 47.7% | Its behaviour | The sizes and times of each response that the Session gave the document, as the TAO check and CORS let the page see them. A Page has no service worker, so the worker's times are 0 and its source types empty |
| `TextEncoder`: `new TextEncoder()`, `encode()`, `encodeInto()` | 57.5% | Its behaviour | UTF-8, as Blink's `TextEncoder` writes it, as [Text encodings](#text-encodings) says |
| `Window`: `requestAnimationFrame()` | 65.9% | Its behaviour | Each callback in the next frame, at the Machine's refresh rate, as [Animation frames](#animation-frames) says |
| `TextDecoder`: `new TextDecoder()`, `decode()` | 45.0% | Its behaviour | Blink's codecs for the Encoding Standard's encodings, as [Text encodings](#text-encodings) says |
| `AbortController`: `new AbortController()`; `AbortSignal`: `timeout()`, `any()`, `throwIfAborted()` | 57.2% | Its behaviour | Signals that `addEventListener()` and `fetch()` follow, as [Abort signals](#abort-signals) says |
| `Window`: `requestIdleCallback()` | 54.6% | Its behaviour | Blink's long idle periods of a page that draws no frames, as [Idle callbacks](#idle-callbacks) says |
| `Window`: `event` | 29.8% | Its behaviour | The event that a listener is called for, and `undefined` otherwise, as Blink sets it around each listener |
| `Scheduler`: `yield()`, `postTask()` | 9.1% | Its behaviour | Tasks of the Page's event loop, in the order of their priorities, as [The scheduler](#the-scheduler) says |
| `LockManager`: `request()`, `query()` | 8.2% | Its behaviour | Web Locks within the Page, as [Web Locks and the Cookie Store](#web-locks-and-the-cookie-store) says |
| `MessageChannel`: `new MessageChannel()`; `MessagePort`: `postMessage()`, `start()`, `close()`; `BroadcastChannel`: `new BroadcastChannel()`, `postMessage()`, `close()`; `Window`: `postMessage()`, `structuredClone()` | 59.4% | Its behaviour | Structured clone and messaging, within the Page and between the Session's Pages, as [Structured clone and messaging](#structured-clone-and-messaging) says |
| `Storage`: `getItem()`, `key()`, `setItem()`, `removeItem()`, `clear()` | 80.6% | Its behaviour | `localStorage` and `sessionStorage`, which the Session keeps for all its Pages, with `storage` events between them, as [localStorage and sessionStorage](#localstorage-and-sessionstorage) says |
| `CookieStore`: `getAll()`, `set()`, `get()`, `delete()` | 7.4% | Its behaviour | The Session's cookies, as `document.cookie` reads and writes them, with change events, as [Web Locks and the Cookie Store](#web-locks-and-the-cookie-store) says |
| `HTMLLinkElement`, `HTMLScriptElement`, `HTMLImageElement`: `fetchPriority` | 44.9% | Its behaviour | An enumerated attribute that reflects its content attribute, `auto` when that is missing or not one of its values, as `[ReflectOnly]` says |
| `HTMLElement`: `autocapitalize` | 1.4% | Its behaviour | Blink's, which reads its content attribute in one of four forms |
| `NamedNodeMap`: `getNamedItem()`, `item()` | 12.9% | Its behaviour | An element's attributes as `Attr` nodes, as [What the DOM has](#what-the-dom-has) says |
| `SVGElement`: `className`; `SVGAElement`, `SVGFEImageElement`, `SVGFilterElement`, `SVGGradientElement`, `SVGImageElement`, `SVGMPathElement`, `SVGPatternElement`, `SVGScriptElement`, `SVGTextPathElement`, `SVGUseElement`: `href`; the 12 `SVGAnimated` interfaces: `animVal` | 6.0% | Its behaviour | SVG's animated values, which read their attributes as Blink parses them and keep what a page sets, as [What the DOM has](#what-the-dom-has) says. As no animation runs, `animVal` gives `baseVal`'s value, or a read-only object of it |
| `CookieChangeEvent`: `changed`, `deleted`; `SecurityPolicyViolationEvent`: `blockedURI` | 11.1% | Its behaviour | An event's constructor sets the attributes that its init dictionary names, as the DOM Standard's inner event creation steps do |
| `CustomEvent`: `initCustomEvent()` | 5.8% | Its behaviour | It sets the event's type, flags and `detail`, as Blink's does |
| `PermissionsPolicy`: `features()`, `allowedFeatures()`, `allowsFeature()`, `getAllowlistForFeature()` | 31.1% | Chrome's outcome | The policy-controlled features of the Page's runtime-enabled features, in the order of Blink's hash map of their names, each allowed by its default allowlist, as for a top-level page without a `Permissions-Policy` header, but `unload` on the pages that the Persona's seed deprecates it on ([persona.md](persona.md#what-a-page-of-each-persona-turns-on-and-off)). Chrome's console warning for a feature or origin it does not know is left out |
| `Document`: `open()` | 3.7% | Its behaviour | With `write()`, `writeln()` and `close()`, as [Loading a document](#loading-a-document) says. With three arguments it calls `window.open()`, or throws Chrome's `InvalidAccessError` for a document without a window |
| `Permissions`: `query()` | 20.8% | Chrome's outcome | A `PermissionStatus` with each name's state in a profile that has granted nothing, `granted` or `prompt`, or Chrome's error for a name it does not support; on a page that is not a secure context, `denied` for every name but `storage-access` |
| `Notification`: `permission`, `requestPermission()`, its constructor | 16.8% | Chrome's outcome | `default`, and `denied` on a page that is not a secure context, as `query()` says for `notifications`. `requestPermission()` waits at Chrome's prompt on a secure page, and gives `denied` elsewhere. `new Notification()` throws Chrome's `TypeError` where the Page's features turn `NotificationConstructor` off, then checks its options as Blink's `Notification::Create()` and `CreateNotificationData()` do, with Chrome's messages, for actions, a `showTrigger` where `NotificationTriggers` is on, a vibration pattern of a silent one and `renotify` without a tag, and serializes `data` for storage, which throws a `DataCloneError` for a function or a `SharedArrayBuffer`. Nobody has granted the permission, so `error` fires at it from a task and it never shows. Its other members are Known Gaps |
| `StorageManager`: `estimate()` | 11.3% | Its behaviour | What the Session's storage holds for the document's storage key, of the Cache API, and Chrome's fixed quota of 10 GiB more, for any disk of at least 10 GiB ([The Cache API](#the-cache-api)). A document that its Session did not load gets no usage. `persisted()` and `persist()` give `false`, as Chrome's durable storage gives a new profile with no engagement |
| `PresentationRequest`: `new PresentationRequest()`, `getAvailability()`, `reconnect()` | 4.4% | Chrome's outcome | The constructor checks its URLs as Blink does. No display to present to: `getAvailability()` gives the same `PresentationAvailability` each time, whose `value` stays `false`, and `reconnect()` rejects with Chrome's `NotFoundError`. `start()`, which needs a user's activation, stays a Known Gap |
| `Document`: `hasStorageAccess()` | 4.4% | Chrome's outcome | `true` for a top-level document on a secure page, whose cookies a new profile allows, and `false` elsewhere |
| `Window`: `webkitRequestFileSystem()` | 4.0% | Chrome's outcome | The success callback gets a `DOMFileSystem` with Chrome's name for the origin and its root directory, from a task of its own, and the error callback Chrome's error for an opaque origin or another type. Reading and changing the file system stay Known Gaps |
| `CredentialsContainer`: `get()` | 3.2% | Chrome's outcome | Blink's checks of each kind of request, then the browser's answer for a profile with no saved password or passkey and no phone: `null` for passwords and federated credentials once the password store is read, one request at a time; a WebAuthn request that waits in Chrome's dialog for a person, who never answers, in the browser's one slot until an abort cancels it, and Chrome's `SecurityError` on an IP address; a WebOTP request that waits for a text until the browser gives up after four minutes; and digital credentials refused for want of a user's activation. A FedCM request that passes Blink's and the browser's first checks waits: the browser then asks the identity provider's servers for its files, which the Runtime does not fetch yet. `create()`, `store()` and `preventSilentAccess()` stay Known Gaps |
| `TrustedTypePolicyFactory`: `getAttributeType()`, `isHTML()`, `isScript()`, `isScriptURL()` | 2.8% | Chrome's outcome | Blink's table of the attributes that take a trusted type, with the Engine Release's event handlers, and whether a value is an object of that type. Making a policy stays a Known Gap |
| `Geolocation`: `getCurrentPosition()` | 2.5% | Chrome's outcome | On a secure page, Chrome's prompt waits and the timeout runs meanwhile; elsewhere the error callback says that only secure origins are allowed |
| `UserActivation`: `hasBeenActive`, `isActive` | 18.4% | Chrome's outcome | `false` and `false`, from a class of the Runtime's, which `postMessage()`'s `includeUserActivation` and `MessageEvent`'s init dictionary take too: a Page has no user activation until M9 |
| `Navigator`: `getGamepads()` | 1.8% | Chrome's outcome | Eight `null`s, Chrome's array with no gamepad |
| `Navigator`: `getBattery()`; `BatteryManager`: `charging`, `level`, `chargingTime`, `dischargingTime` | 17.6% | Chrome's outcome | A desktop with no battery, as the Machine claims: `charging` `true`, `level` 1, `chargingTime` 0 and `dischargingTime` `Infinity`, from the same promise each time, which resolves from a task of its own |
| `MediaDevices`: `enumerateDevices()` | 14.5% | Chrome's outcome | One entry of each kind of device that the Machine has, `audioinput`, `videoinput` and `audiooutput` in that order, with an empty ID, group and label, as Chrome gives a page that may not capture. The Machine's `audio_input`, `video_input` and `audio_output` say which ([api.md](api.md#machine)) |
| `Keyboard`: `getLayoutMap()` | 9.1% | Chrome's outcome | The US layout, which Windows sets up for the locale `en-US` of every Persona: 48 keys, in the order of Blink's `HashMap` of them, as Chrome 157 gave it on Windows |
| `Navigator`: `cpuPerformance` | 7.0% | Chrome's outcome | Chrome's tier of the Machine's processors, 1 to 4, for an AMD or Intel CPU of none of the models that Chrome singles out |
| `Document`: `prerendering` | 62.5% | Its default | `false`: a Page is never prerendered |
| `Window`: `scrollX`, `scrollY`, `pageXOffset`, `pageYOffset` | 59.0% | Its default | 0: nothing scrolls without layout and input. Each is `[Replaceable]`, so a page can set it |
| `Element`, `Document`: `getAnimations()` | 5.4% | Its default | `[]`: no animation runs without style |
| `MediaSession`: `setActionHandler()`, `setPositionState()`, `metadata`, `playbackState` | 2.8% | Its default | Nothing that a page sees but the checks of its arguments, and what a setter stores |
| `StorageManager`: `getDirectory()` | 2.5% | Its default | A `FileSystemDirectoryHandle` |
| `Window`: `focus()` | 2.0% | Its default | Nothing that a top-level page sees |
| `Document`: `webkitIsFullScreen`, `pictureInPictureElement`, `activeViewTransition` | 1.7% | Its default | `false` and `null`: nothing is fullscreen, in picture-in-picture or in a view transition |
| `Scheduling`: `isInputPending()` | 1.6% | Its default | `false`: no input is pending until M9 |
| `SpeechRecognition`: `new SpeechRecognition()` | 1.3% | Its default | An object of the interface, as Chrome's |
| `Navigator`: `sendBeacon()` | 52.8% | Its behaviour | A keepalive POST of the Request Kind `beacon`, as [Fetch](#fetch) says |
| `Window`: `fetchLater()` | 3.1% | Its behaviour | A keepalive fetch that the Session sends later, as [Fetch](#fetch) says |
| `XMLHttpRequest`: `responseXML` | 5.0% | Its default until M8 | A Request Kind of its own |
| `History`: `replaceState()`, `pushState()` | 43.0% | Its behaviour | The tab's session history ([Session history](#session-history)) |
| `IDBFactory`, `IDBDatabase`, `IDBObjectStore`, `IDBCursor`: 22 members | 27.7% | Its behaviour | The Session's databases, which its Pages share ([IndexedDB](#indexeddb)) |
| `Cache`: 7 members; `CacheStorage`: `keys()`, `has()`, `match()`, `delete()` | 19.8% | Its behaviour | The Session's caches, which its Pages share ([The Cache API](#the-cache-api)) |
| `Element`: `attachShadow()`; `CustomElementRegistry`: `define()`; `HTMLElement`: `focusGroup` | 22.3% | Its default until M8 | Shadow trees and custom elements, with the rest of the DOM. `attachShadow()` is refused, so it throws until then. `focusGroup` is behind a feature that is off |
| `Element`: `getBoundingClientRect()`, `getClientRects()`, `checkVisibility()`, `computedStyleMap()`; `Range`: `getBoundingClientRect()`, `getClientRects()`; `Document`: `elementFromPoint()`, `elementsFromPoint()`, `caretPositionFromPoint()` | 70.2% | Its default until M9 | Layout and hit testing |
| `IntersectionObserver`, `ResizeObserver`: their constructors | 57.1% | Its default until M9 | Layout |
| `MouseEvent`: 12 members; `PointerEvent`: 14 members; `WheelEvent`: `deltaMode`; `TextEvent`: `data` | 44.0% | Its default until M9 | Events of input. A page's own event gets what its init dictionary gives, as `CookieChangeEvent`'s above, but for the attributes that Chrome works out, such as `x` and `altitudeAngle` |
| `HTMLElement`: `innerText`, `attributeStyleMap`; `SVGElement`, `MathMLElement`: `attributeStyleMap` | 43.2% | Its default until M9 | Style and layout |
| `HTMLElement`: `showPopover()` | at least 1% | Its behaviour | The popover API, as [What the DOM has](#what-the-dom-has) says |
| `Element`: `animate()`; `Animation`: 12 members | 13.1% | Its default until M9 | Web Animations, which need style |
| `Document`, `ShadowRoot`: `adoptedStyleSheets`; `CSSStyleSheet`: `replaceSync()`, `rules`; `CSSStyleRule`: `styleMap`; `CSS`: `registerProperty()` | 12.5% | Its default until M9 | The CSSOM. `adoptedStyleSheets` is already a new page's `[]` |
| `FontFace`: `new FontFace()` | 10.6% | Its default until M9 | Fonts |
| `Selection`: 12 members | 9.8% | Its default until M9 | Ranges and layout |
| `Document`: `startViewTransition()` | 1.2% | Its default until M9 | Rendering |
| `WebGLRenderingContext`, `WebGL2RenderingContext`: `getParameter()`, `getExtension()`, `getSupportedExtensions()`, `getShaderPrecisionFormat()`; their WebGPU forms: `getParameter()`, `getShaderPrecisionFormat()` | 27.6% | Its default until M10 | WebGL, with a GPU that the Machine claims |
| `SubtleCrypto`: `digest()`, `importKey()`, `encrypt()`, `decrypt()`, `exportKey()`, `generateKey()`, `deriveKey()`, `sign()` | 22.7% | Its default until M10 | WebCrypto on BoringSSL. These are refused, so they throw until then |
| `CanvasRenderingContext2D`, `OffscreenCanvasRenderingContext2D`: 6 members each; `OffscreenCanvas`: `new OffscreenCanvas()`, `transferToImageBitmap()` | 13.9% | Its default until M10 | Canvas 2D |
| `HTMLCanvasElement`: `getContext()`, `toDataURL()`, `transferControlToOffscreen()` | 47.0% | Its default until M10 | A canvas's contexts. `getContext()` gives null until then |
| `BaseAudioContext`, `AudioContext`, `OfflineAudioContext`, `AudioNode`, `AudioBuffer`, `AudioParam`, `AnalyserNode`: 17 members | 8.4% | Its default until M10 | Web Audio |
| `MediaCapabilities`: `decodingInfo()` | 5.7% | Its default until M10 | Whether a decoder is power-efficient reads the GPU that M10's Machine claims |
| `CookieStoreManager`: `getSubscriptions()`, `subscribe()`, `unsubscribe()` | 11.1% | Its default until a milestone has it | Service workers' behaviour, which the roadmap leaves out |
| `RTCIceCandidate`: `candidate`; `RTCSessionDescription`: `sdp`; `RTCPeerConnection`: `addTransceiver()` | 3.6% | Its default until a milestone has it | WebRTC, which the roadmap leaves out |
| `HTMLVideoElement`: 4 members; `HTMLMediaElement`: `srcObject` | 5.2% | Its default until a milestone has it | Media playback, which the roadmap leaves out |
| `Document`: `evaluate()`; `XPathEvaluator`: `new XPathEvaluator()`, `createExpression()` | 4.5% | Its default until a milestone has it | XPath, which no milestone has yet |
| `URLPattern`: `new URLPattern()` | 2.9% | Its default until a milestone has it | URL patterns, which no milestone has yet |
| `Document`: `requestStorageAccess()` | 2.6% | Its default until a milestone has it | It resolves with `undefined`, as Chrome's does for a top-level document on a secure page. Elsewhere Chrome rejects with a `NotAllowedError`, a gap until its second overload, which gives a `StorageAccessHandle`, has a class |
| `PerformanceEventTiming`: `interactionId` | 31.1% | Nothing | Its entries come from input |
| `GeolocationCoordinates`: `latitude`, `longitude`, `accuracy`, `heading`, `speed` | 1.8% | Nothing | No position is given without a grant |

### What the bindings run on

The generated callbacks and tables call a small runtime in [`native/bindings`](../native/bindings), on V8's own API, as Blink's `platform/bindings`:

- A Page's global object gets each interface object, namespace object, `[LegacyWindowAlias]` and `[LegacyFactoryFunction]`, such as `Image`, as a lazy data property that is not enumerable, as Blink's `IDLMemberInstaller::InstallExposedConstructs()` installs them: V8 makes the object the first time a page reads it, and the property is a data property from then on ([`idl_member_installer.h`](../native/bindings/idl_member_installer.h)). Those that are always there go on the template of the global object, and V8 installs a template's lazy data properties before its other properties, last first, as it does in Chrome. So `Object.getOwnPropertyNames(window)` lists V8's builtins, then the interface objects from `Option`, `Image` and `Audio` back to `AbortController`, then the members of `Window`, then what the context decides, as Chrome's does.
- Each context makes an interface object once, with its parent's for its own prototype, and installs on it and its prototype what the context decides, before a wrapper of the interface or anything else can reach them, as Blink's `V8PerContextData::ConstructorForType()` does. A namespace object is made each time its property is read, which happens once.
- The Runtime's C++ objects live on V8's C++ heap, cppgc, on which Blink's Oilpan is built, so that V8 collects them with the JavaScript objects that refer to them ([`script_wrappable.h`](../native/bindings/script_wrappable.h)). A wrapper holds its object through `v8::Object::Wrap()`, with the type tag that Blink's Web IDL database gives the interface, and the object holds its wrapper through a traced reference. A listener that a page adds is held the same way, so a target, its listeners and their closures go together when nothing else holds them.
- An argument converts to an object of an interface only if V8's template of that interface, or of one that inherits from it, made it, and its tag lies in the interface's range.
- What each context decides is installed once its interface objects exist: the `@@unscopables` of a prototype, the members of a secure context, which the origin of the context's document decides, and for a frame's its parent's being one too, so the Page's first context, whose about:blank has an opaque origin, is none, as a new tab's about:blank is none in Chrome, and those behind a feature that a context could turn on, which a Page has as its set says, since it has no origin trials. Each document that a Page loads gets a context made for it, so one at a URL that is not potentially trustworthy, such as `http://www.example.test/`, has none of the members of a secure context. Whether a context is a secure context, and the time origin that its `performance.now()` counts from, are the context's own, kept with it ([`per_context_data.h`](../native/bindings/per_context_data.h)), as Blink keeps them for each window and worker, since a Page's frames each have a context in one isolate.
- A call of a member makes the wrappers that it returns in the relevant realm of its receiver, as Blink's bindings convert a return value with the receiver's `ScriptState`: so `frame.contentDocument.body` is a node of the frame's realm, whichever context asks. A constructor or a static member, which has no receiver, makes them in the current context, and so does a member that another origin may call on a window, so that no script that runs within the call gets an object of the window's realm. A `DOMException` that a call throws is of the current realm too, whatever its receiver's, as Blink's `V8ThrowDOMException` makes it. Contexts of one origin share V8's security token, as Blink's `LocalWindowProxy::SetSecurityToken()` gives them, so that each reaches the others' objects. A context of an opaque origin keeps its own, but one whose document took the opaque origin of another's, as a frame's first document and a srcdoc take their parent's and an about:blank its initiator's, shares that one's, as Blink's `CanAccess()` lets an opaque origin reach itself, so `frame.contentWindow.document` of a frame in about:blank is the frame's document. V8 lets nothing reach a context of another origin, opaque or not, until the checks of [#286](https://github.com/darionxt/mitto/issues/286) let some members through: a property of such a window throws V8's `no access`, and so does a member of the caller's own window that it calls on such a window, such as the getter of `document`, since V8 checks each member's receiver, as Blink's `IDLMemberInstaller` has it check, but that of a static member or of a `[CrossOrigin]` one, and checks `location` too until a `Location` has checks of its own. A window that its frame navigated away from loses its security token, and V8 asks `Window::AccessCheckCallback()` what it may access, as it asks Blink's: a window of its own origin. Its scripts' bare members are those of the frame's current window, if they may access it, and it has no named properties, as in Chrome. Known Gaps: so is its `document`, where Chrome's is the document it had; and a callback that outlives its page, such as that of `Atomics.waitAsync()` in a frame or of a `FinalizationRegistry`, still runs, and may access the next document if it is of its origin, which Chrome's does not reach.
- An interface whose class has an indexed or a named property getter, such as `PluginArray`, makes Web IDL's legacy platform objects: V8 asks interceptors on its instance template for their indexed and named properties, as Blink's do ([`indexed_and_named_properties.h`](../native/bindings/indexed_and_named_properties.h)). So `navigator.plugins[0]` and `navigator.plugins["PDF Viewer"]` are own properties, read-only, the named ones not enumerable under `[LegacyUnenumerableNamedProperties]`, and listed after the indices. A named property never hides a property of the object or its prototypes, but under `[LegacyOverrideBuiltIns]`, as `DOMStringMap`'s hide even `toString`. Setting, defining or deleting one fails as Web IDL says, quietly in sloppy code and with Chrome's error in strict code, such as "Failed to set an indexed property [0] on 'PluginArray': Indexed property setter is not supported.", unless the class has a property setter or deleter: then setting or defining such a property calls the setter, which throws "Accessor properties are not allowed." for an accessor, and an index has a writable property, as `SVGNumberList`'s do; deleting a named one calls the deleter, as `bind_gen`'s interceptors do. An indexed getter that may throw, such as `SVGNumberList`'s `getItem()`, is called only below the length, so an index past the end gives `undefined`, as in Chrome. The prototype's `@@iterator` is `%Array.prototype.values%`. The generator refuses, by name, an indexed setter without an indexed getter, a named setter or deleter without a named getter, and the named properties of a global object, until a class needs them.
- The bindings of a class cannot pass it an object of an interface without a class, so a union in a member with behaviour loses such members, and an overload that needs one is never chosen: `(DOMString or TrustedScript)` converts as `DOMString` does, and `setAttribute()` has one overload. Overloads that differ in how many arguments they take are chosen by that number. A Known Gap converts to such an object as to any other.
- An error's message is the error itself, such as "1 argument required, but only 0 present.". As in Chrome, the context of the call goes in front of it as it leaves the call, through V8's exception propagation callback ([`exception_context.h`](../native/bindings/exception_context.h)): "Failed to execute 'addEventListener' on 'EventTarget': ", and for each dictionary being converted, "Failed to read the 'signal' property from 'AddEventListenerOptions': ". V8 calls it for an error thrown directly in the call, so an error that V8 throws there, such as converting a Symbol to a string, gets the context too, and one that a page's own getter throws does not.

### Runtime-enabled features

Blink puts many interfaces and members behind runtime-enabled features, which [`runtime_enabled_features.json5`](https://source.chromium.org/chromium/chromium/src/+/main:third_party/blink/renderer/platform/runtime_enabled_features.json5) lists. A Page has a set of features when it starts, and installs what is behind each one when it builds its templates, as Blink does, so that a Persona of an older Chrome Release gives it the set of its release ([ADR-0008](adr/0008-v8-and-bindings-from-web-idl.md)).

By default a Page has the Engine Release's set for stable on Windows, which the generator works out from that file as Blink's build and Chrome's startup work it out ([`runtime_features.py`](../native/bindings/bindings_generator/runtime_features.py)): a feature is on if its status for Windows is `stable`, or if the base feature that Chrome copies into it is on by default. When a page's binding asks, a feature that it depends on and that is off turns it off, and one that implies it and is on turns it on. Field trials come from the Persona, below. What Chrome's own code turns on beside the file is not counted, and the [Parity Checks of the Script Fingerprint](parity.md#the-script-fingerprint) show where that differs, but for what Chrome's content layer or Chrome itself sets when a renderer starts, where a page shows it: `LocalNetworkAccessPermissionPolicy`, from `kLocalNetworkAccessChecks`, which gives `Request` its `targetAddressSpace`, `PaymentRequest` and `PaymentApp`, from `kWebPayments` and `kServiceWorkerPaymentApps`, which expose `PaymentRequest` and `PaymentManager` with what goes with them, `SerialPortConnected`, from `device::features::kSerialPortConnected`, which gives `SerialPort` its `connected`, `TranslateService`, which Chrome's default preferences turn on and `HrefTranslate` depends on, for the `hrefTranslate` of `HTMLAnchorElement`, `WebShare`, which Chrome turns on on Windows, for `navigator.share()` and `canShare()`, and `LegacyAbstractRange`, which the content layer turns off as the inverse of `OpaqueRange`, so that `AbstractRange.prototype` has no `startContainer`.

A Page that the library starts turns on and then off the features that the `features` of its [start message](#the-messages) name, which come from its Persona: so a Page of an older Chrome Release lacks what that release did not ship yet, and each Persona's Page has what its variations seed switches, as [persona.md](persona.md#what-a-page-of-each-persona-turns-on-and-off) says. The same `features` give the V8 flags that the Page sets after the Engine Release's, before V8 starts.

Given a script, the Page program takes Chrome's options for the set instead, before the script: `--enable-blink-features=A,B` and `--disable-blink-features=C`, each a list of names separated by commas, which turn those features on and then off, so a feature that both name ends up off, as in Chrome. It refuses a name that is not a feature, and either option with `--session=`.

```text
$ out/runtime/mitto_page "'hasUAVisualTransition' in PopStateEvent.prototype"
true
$ out/runtime/mitto_page --disable-blink-features=HasUAVisualTransition "'hasUAVisualTransition' in PopStateEvent.prototype"
false
```

### Add an interface

An interface that Chrome exposes is on a Page already, from its table, with every member a Known Gap, so that its shape is Chrome's. A new Engine Release's interface gets a table with no change here, unless it uses a construct, a type or an extended attribute that the generator does not know, which stops the build with its name. To give an interface behaviour, give it a C++ class, which takes the place of its table:

1. Write its class in `native/dom/<file>.h` and `.cc`, where `<file>` is the interface's name in lowercase words, such as `pop_state_event` for `PopStateEvent`, or in a header that holds several classes, which its entry's `header` names, as `svg_elements.h` holds the classes of SVG that have nothing of their own yet: `mitto::<interface>`, which derives from `bindings::ScriptWrappable`, or from its parent interface's class, and returns its generated `bindings::V8<interface>::kWrapperTypeInfo` from `GetWrapperTypeInfo()`. It has a static `Create()` with the constructor's arguments, if the interface has one, and a method for each member, named after the member, or after its `[ImplementedAs]`, with its first letter in upper case: `Type()` for an attribute `type`, `SetCancelBubble()` for its setter, and `InitEvent()` for an operation `initEvent`. A method takes the arguments of the IDL, converted: `bool`, `const std::u16string&` for a `DOMString`, a pointer to the class of an interface, a generated struct for a dictionary, `std::optional` for an optional argument without a default. One whose member is `[RaisesException]` takes a `bindings::ExceptionState&` last. A class with an indexed property getter also has `Length()`, whose supported property indices are those below it, and one with a named property getter has `NamedPropertyEnumerator()`, its supported property names in order, and `NamedPropertyQuery()`, whether a name is one, as Blink's classes have. A getter that finds nothing returns null, or an empty `std::optional` for a `DOMString`. A named property setter takes the name and the converted value, and a deleter the name, returning whether it deleted the property; one without an identifier is `AnonymousNamedSetter()` or `AnonymousNamedDeleter()`, as in Blink. The generator writes the interceptors that call them. A static attribute is a static method; with `[SaveSameObject]` the bindings keep what it gives first on the interface object, as Chrome's keep `PerformanceObserver.supportedEntryTypes`. A class whose read-only maplike or setlike declaration has behaviour has `MaplikeEntries()` or `SetlikeValues()`, its entries in order, from which the generator writes `size`, `get()`, `has()`, `forEach()` and the iterators of `entries()`, `keys()` and `values()`, and one whose pair iterable has behaviour has `CreateIterationSource()`, which makes a `bindings::PairIterationSource` that gives each pair in turn. A getter may return null for an interface type that the IDL does not make nullable, as Blink's classes do. A `[LegacyFactoryFunction]`, such as `Image`, that the entry names by its name has its behaviour in the class's static `CreateForJSConstructor()`, which takes its arguments. A stringifier attribute's `toString()` gives what its getter gives, and the setter of a `[PutForwards]` attribute sets the attribute it names on what the getter gives, through V8, so that a page's own getters run, as `bind_gen`'s do.
2. Add it to `mitto_bindings_interfaces` in [`native/bindings/interfaces.gni`](../native/bindings/interfaces.gni), with the members whose behaviour it does not have yet in `missing`, or those it has in `implemented`, where `indexed getter` and `named getter` name those operations, and `maplike` or `setlike` the declaration, and its files to [`native/dom/BUILD.gn`](../native/dom/BUILD.gn). Its parent interface needs a class first. Mark it `made_by_known_gaps = true`, and give the class a static `CreateForKnownGap(info)`, which `bindings::MakeForKnownGap()` helps write, so that the Known Gaps that give its objects keep giving them ([What a Known Gap does](#what-a-known-gap-does)): otherwise the generator refuses them. Its constructor is then a member named `constructor`, which may stay a Known Gap that makes the object that way, as `new CustomElementRegistry()` does.
3. Build the Runtime. If the C++ writer refuses a type, an extended attribute or a construct that the interface uses, teach it in `cpp_types.py` or `cpp_writer.py`, following `bind_gen` for what a page sees.
4. Run the [Parity Checks of the Script Fingerprint](parity.md#the-script-fingerprint) and write their list again: a class keeps its interface's shape, so it adds no gap of the kind `shape`, and the gaps of its behaviour that it closes leave the list. Add a case of what it does to [`tests/page_cases.js`](../tests/page_cases.js), with what the Engine Release's Chrome gives for it in [`tests/data/page_cases.json`](../tests/data/page_cases.json), or a test of its own, and run the tests.

### What the tests compare with Chrome

What a page sees of every interface, the global object's own properties among it, in order, with their attributes, names and `length`s, the prototypes, and what each member throws when it is called on an object of another kind, is compared with real Chrome by the [Parity Checks of the Script Fingerprint](parity.md#the-script-fingerprint), against Baselines of each Chrome Release of the Supported Window that each run records ([releasing.md](releasing.md#4-record-baselines)).

How it behaves is compared with real Chrome by [web-platform-tests](wpt.md): each testharness.js test of the directories that M8 is held to runs in a Page, against Windows Chrome's results for the Engine Release, which each run records too, and CI holds the differences to a list.

[`tests/test_page_bindings.py`](../tests/test_page_bindings.py) runs [`tests/page_cases.js`](../tests/page_cases.js) in the Page program, for what no Baseline holds: the own properties of an instance of each class that the Runtime implements, such as an `Event`'s `isTrusted`, which the walk reaches none of, and the outcome of some calls, such as a dispatch to listeners or a call with an argument of the wrong type. It compares them with what Chrome 157.0.8103.39 gave for the same script, which [`tests/data/page_cases.json`](../tests/data/page_cases.json) keeps. M6 recorded that in Chrome on Windows, with that script and the comparison of the global object and the interfaces that the Parity Checks took over, and no run records it again: a new case takes its outcome from the Engine Release's Chrome by hand.

### Tests of the bindings

| Test | What it checks | Where it runs |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The build of `//mitto:runtime` | The generator reads the Engine Release's whole database without refusing anything, and `generator_test` checks that it refuses [`native/bindings/test/unknown_extended_attribute.idl`](../native/bindings/test/unknown_extended_attribute.idl), whose extended attribute no release has, with a message that names it | CI's native job on every Host, which builds the Runtime |
| [`tests/test_page_bindings.py`](../tests/test_page_bindings.py) | The own properties of an instance of each class, and what the cases of `tests/page_cases.js` give, are what Chrome 157.0.8103.39 gave; dispatch works; listeners outlive garbage collection; a Page without `HasUAVisualTransition` lacks `PopStateEvent.prototype.hasUAVisualTransition`; the options of the feature set; what `navigator.plugins`, `navigator.mimeTypes` and a plugin give through their indexed and named properties, keys, descriptors and errors among them, is what Chrome 157 gave for the same expressions | The native job on every Host, with the Page program from the installed wheel, and the Runtime's builds with AddressSanitizer and UndefinedBehaviorSanitizer |
| [`tests/test_page_known_gaps.py`](../tests/test_page_known_gaps.py) | What a Known Gap does, for each kind of member: the number of arguments, overloads, conversions and their messages, and the receiver of a promise, each as Chrome 157 gave it; the defaults, the same object on one receiver, what a setter stores, constructors, objects with an `EventTarget` or `Event` behind them, event handlers, and refusals | The native job on every Host, and the Runtime's builds with AddressSanitizer and UndefinedBehaviorSanitizer |
| [`tests/test_page_common_members.py`](../tests/test_page_common_members.py) | What the members that pages call most give and throw, for those that have their behaviour or Chrome's outcome: `crypto.getRandomValues()`, `crypto.randomUUID()`, `QuotaExceededError`, `atob()` and `btoa()`, events from their init dictionaries, `CustomEvent`, the reflected attributes that keywords limit, `autocapitalize`, `Notification`, `permissions.query()`, `getGamepads()`, `hasStorageAccess()`, `navigator.storage`, `document.featurePolicy`'s lists, `trustedTypes`' table and checks and `PresentationRequest` on a secure page, each as Chrome 157 gave it; the US layout of `keyboard.getLayoutMap()` as Chrome 157 gave it on Windows; `getBattery()`'s desktop with no battery; geolocation as headful Chrome 157 on Windows gave it; that random values differ from call to call; that `requestPermission()` waits at the prompt; and a watch's timeout and the clamping of its options | The native job on every Host, and the Runtime's builds with AddressSanitizer and UndefinedBehaviorSanitizer |
| [`tests/test_page_reachable_objects.py`](../tests/test_page_reachable_objects.py) | What [a top-level page reads](#what-a-top-level-page-reads) of its window and document, and of the objects they give, with the same objects on every read, each as Chrome 157 gave it; the objects that a Known Gap makes of a class; and that the probe reaches in a Page every object that it reached in Chrome, by the Engine Release's Baseline | The native job on every Host, and the Runtime's builds with AddressSanitizer and UndefinedBehaviorSanitizer |
| [`tests/test_bindings_generator.py`](../tests/test_bindings_generator.py) | The install plan's order, the names in C++, the stable set of features, the listing, refusals, the interceptors of indexed and named property getters, named setters and deleters, and that GN's `.pydeps` name every module of the generator | Every job that runs the tests, with no build |

## The document

A Page's scripts get a DOM of the Runtime's own, in [`native/dom`](../native/dom), whose classes follow Blink's, with Blink's checks and messages, and whose bindings the generator writes. Its nodes live on V8's C++ heap, as Blink's do on Oilpan: a node holds its parent, children and siblings with members on that heap, and its wrapper holds it through V8's wrapping, so a node and its wrapper live and die together, a detached tree lives as long as a script holds any of its nodes, and what a script put on a node's wrapper stays while the node lives.

### The window

The global object is a `Window`, made from `Window`'s template as Blink makes a window's context, so `window`, `self` and `globalThis` are the global proxy, its prototype chain is Chrome's, `Window.prototype`, then `WindowProperties` and `EventTarget.prototype`, and each is an immutable prototype, as Web IDL has it. `window`, `self` and `document` have their behaviour, and so do `navigator`, `screen`, the window's place and size below, and what [a top-level page reads](#what-a-top-level-page-reads) of its window and document. `WindowProperties` finds the window's child frames by name first, then the document's elements by ID, and its images, forms, embeds and objects by name, as `window[name]` and a bare name do ([Frames](#frames)). The document's wrapper has a property of each named item, as Chrome's does: forms, iframes, embeds and objects by name, objects by ID, and images by name, or by ID while they have a name. `Window`'s other members are Known Gaps.

What a page reads of the browser and the machine is what its Session sends, worked out by the same code or from the same data ([persona.md](persona.md#the-runtime)):

| A page reads | What it gives |
| --- | --- |
| `navigator.userAgent`, `appVersion` | The Session's `User-Agent`, and that without `Mozilla/` |
| `navigator.platform` | `Win32`, as Chrome on Windows gives, for a Persona of Windows |
| `navigator.language`, `languages` | The languages of the Session's `Accept-Language`, without their weights, as Chrome's language setting gives both, through Blink's `ParseAndSanitize()`, and the Persona's locale for none. `languages` is the same frozen array each time |
| `navigator.userAgentData` | A new object each time, as in Chrome. `brands`, `mobile`, `platform`, `toJSON()` and `getHighEntropyValues()`, which resolves from a task of its own, are the values of the Session's user agent client hints, read back from the Persona's own lines, with the Machine's platform version |
| `navigator.hardwareConcurrency`, `deviceMemory`, `maxTouchPoints` | The Machine's processors, memory and touch points, as `device-memory` sends the memory |
| `navigator.connection` | `effectiveType` as `ect` says, and the `rtt` and `downlink` that the renderer process of the document's site gives its host, which the library works out with the code of a subresource's hints. So it is what a `fetch()` from the document to its own host sends. about:blank's host is empty, and gets noise too, as in Chrome. `saveData` is `false` |
| `navigator.plugins`, `mimeTypes` | Chrome's list for a browser that views PDFs, as in a new profile: five plugins, `PDF Viewer`, `Chrome PDF Viewer`, `Chromium PDF Viewer`, `Microsoft Edge PDF Viewer` and `WebKit built-in PDF`, each `internal-pdf-viewer` with the mime types `application/pdf` and `text/pdf`, and those two of the first plugin. Each is the same object each time, but a plugin's own mime types, which are new on each read, as in Chrome. `refresh()` is a Known Gap: in Chrome, `refresh(true)` reloads the page |
| `navigator.cookieEnabled`, `onLine`, `pdfViewerEnabled`, `webdriver`, `javaEnabled()`, `doNotTrack`, `vendor`, `product` and the rest of `NavigatorID` | What Chrome gives in a new profile: `true`, `true`, `true`, `false`, `false`, `null`, `Google Inc.`, `Gecko` |
| `screen.width`, `height` | The display over its scale, enclosed |
| `screen.availLeft`, `availTop`, `availWidth`, `availHeight` | The work area |
| `screen.colorDepth`, `pixelDepth`, `isExtended` | `24`, a display that is not HDR, and `false`, one display |
| `screen.orientation` | `portrait-primary` for a display at least as high as wide, and `landscape-primary` otherwise, at the angle 0. `lock()` rejects with Chrome on Windows' `NotSupportedError`, from a task of its own, and `unlock()` does nothing |
| `innerWidth`, `innerHeight` | The viewport |
| `outerWidth`, `outerHeight`, `screenX`, `screenY`, `screenLeft`, `screenTop` | The window's size and place |
| `devicePixelRatio` | The display's scale |

None of these values changes while a Page runs, so no `change` event comes, and the `onchange` handlers of `Screen`, `ScreenOrientation` and `NetworkInformation` are kept but never run. The window's document is about:blank's, `<html><head></head><body></body></html>` in quirks mode, until the Page loads a document through its Session. The Page program given a script takes the document's markup with `--html=MARKUP`, and the script from standard input for `-`.

#### What a top-level page reads

A Page is a tab's top-level page that a Session opened, so its window and document give what Chrome 157 gives such a page, as the Script Fingerprint's Baselines recorded it ([#215](https://github.com/darionxt/mitto/issues/215)). Each object that the window gives is the same object on every read, as in Chrome, but for `viewport.segments`:

| A page reads | What it gives |
| --- | --- |
| `top`, `parent`, `frames`, `length`, `frameElement`, `name`, `closed` | The window's place in its frame's tree ([Frames](#frames)): a top-level window's `top` and `parent` are the window, `frameElement` `null` and `name` empty until a script sets it. `opener` is `null`, and setting `opener` to anything but `null` shadows it with a data property, as Blink's setter does. `open()` opens no other window ([Navigations a page starts](#navigations-a-page-starts)) |
| `origin`, `location` and its parts, `document.location`, `document.domain` | Those of the document's URL: `null` and nothing for about:blank's opaque origin. `document.location` is the window's `location`, whose setters, `assign()`, `replace()` and `reload()` navigate, as [Navigations a page starts](#navigations-a-page-starts) says |
| `isSecureContext`, `originAgentCluster`, `offscreenBuffering`, `crossOriginIsolated` | Whether the context is a secure one, `true`, `true`, and whether it is cross-origin isolated ([Cross-origin isolation](#cross-origin-isolation)) |
| `history` | `length` 1 and `state` `null`, a tab's first document, `scrollRestoration` `auto`, and then the tab's session history, as [Session history](#session-history) says |
| `navigation` | For a document of an origin that is not opaque, the entries of its origin, with random keys, the document's first: one, index 0, with an `activation` whose `navigationType` is `replace`, as a tab's first navigation replaces its initial about:blank. None for about:blank, as in Chrome. `navigate()`, `reload()` and the `navigate` event are as [Navigations a page starts](#navigations-a-page-starts) says, and traversing as [Session history](#session-history) says |
| `locationbar`, `menubar`, `personalbar`, `scrollbars`, `statusbar`, `toolbar` | Each its own object, `visible` |
| `visualViewport` | The viewport, unzoomed and unscrolled: offsets 0, scale 1, and the viewport's width and height, since a Page lays out nothing that would overflow it |
| `viewport.segments` | A new frozen array of one new `DOMRect`, the whole viewport, on each read |
