| `Headers` | `new Headers()` from pairs or a record, `append()`, `set()`, `delete()`, `get()`, `has()` and `getSetCookie()`, with Chrome's checks and guards. It iterates over its lines sorted by name and combined, each `Set-Cookie` on its own, which an iterator sorts and combines again after each change, keeping its place, as Blink's `HeadersIterationSource` does |
| `FormData` | Blink's entries, each a name and a string or a `File`: a `Blob` that goes in becomes a `File` named `blob`, or the name given, of now, and a `File` with a name given a copy of it so named. `append()`, `set()`, which keeps the first entry's place, `delete()`, `get()`, `getAll()`, `has()` and its iterators. `new FormData(form)` is a Known Gap until forms build their entry lists ([#281](https://github.com/darionxt/mitto/issues/281)): it gives an empty `FormData`, whatever the form and the submitter |
| `Request` | `new Request()` with a string, a buffer, a `Blob` or a stream for its body, `method`, `url`, `headers`, `signal`, `duplex`, `body`, `bodyUsed`, `text()`, `json()`, `arrayBuffer()`, `bytes()`, `blob()` and `textStream()` |
| `Response` | `new Response()` with a string, a buffer, a `Blob` or a stream, `Response.json()`, `status`, `ok`, `statusText`, `url`, `redirected`, `type`, `headers`, `body`, `bodyUsed`, `text()`, `json()`, `arrayBuffer()`, `bytes()`, `blob()` and `textStream()` |
| `Blob`, `File`, `FileReader`, `FileReaderSync`, `ProgressEvent` | Every member, as [The File API](#the-file-api) says |
| `HTMLHtmlElement`, `HTMLHeadElement`, `HTMLTitleElement`, `HTMLBaseElement`, `HTMLMetaElement`, `HTMLLinkElement`, `HTMLStyleElement`, `HTMLScriptElement`, `HTMLBodyElement`, `HTMLDivElement`, `HTMLSpanElement`, `HTMLParagraphElement`, `HTMLHeadingElement`, `HTMLBRElement`, `HTMLUListElement`, `HTMLOListElement`, `HTMLLIElement`, `HTMLPreElement`, `HTMLTemplateElement`, `HTMLUnknownElement` | Their reflected attributes, `click()`, as [Events](#events) says, `text` of a script and a title, a script's `src`, `async`, `textContent` and `HTMLScriptElement.supports()`, and a base element's `href`, as [Loading a document](#loading-a-document) says, what a link and a style element load, as [Links and the document's resources](#links-and-the-documents-resources) says, and a template's `content`, a fragment of an inert document of the template's document, which cloning and moving the template carry along, as the HTML Standard says. `new HTMLDivElement()` and the like throw "Illegal constructor", as in Chrome with no custom element defined |
| `HTMLAnchorElement`, `HTMLAreaElement` | Their reflected attributes, and the parts of the URL that `href` resolves to, `href`, `toString()`, `origin`, `protocol`, `username`, `password`, `host`, `hostname`, `port`, `pathname`, `search` and `hash`, with their setters, which change that part and write the URL back into `href`. A URL that does not resolve is none, as Blink's `Url()` has it, and the setters follow `KURL`'s on the components that `//url` parses (`native/dom/resolved_url.h`). A `URL` reads and sets its parts the same way, as Blink's `UrlUtils` gives both (`native/dom/url_utils.h`) |
| `HTMLImageElement`, `HTMLCanvasElement` | Their reflected attributes, and `width` and `height`: the attribute's non-negative integer, or else an image's natural size at a density of 1, 0 before it loads, and a canvas's 300 by 150. `new Image(width, height)` makes an `img` with those attributes, as Blink's does. An `img` loads its source, and has `currentSrc`, `complete`, `naturalWidth`, `naturalHeight` and `decode()`, as [Images](#images) says |
| `HTMLMediaElement`, `HTMLAudioElement`, `HTMLVideoElement` | Their reflected attributes, and `preload`, as Blink reads it. `new Audio(src)` makes an `audio` whose `preload` is `auto`. Playing media stays out |
| `HTMLTableElement`, `HTMLTableSectionElement`, `HTMLTableRowElement`, `HTMLTableCellElement`, `HTMLTableColElement` | Their reflected attributes, and the parts of a table as Blink finds them among the children: `caption`, `tHead`, `tFoot`, their setters and `create`/`delete` methods, `tBodies`, `createTBody()`, and `rows` in Blink's order, the rows of the heads, then those of the table and its bodies, then those of the feet. `insertRow()`, `deleteRow()`, a section's `rows`, a row's `cells`, `insertCell()`, `deleteCell()`, `rowIndex`, `sectionRowIndex` and `cellIndex` follow Blink's, with its messages, and the spans are clamped as Blink clamps them, 1 to 1000 columns and 0 to 65534 rows, or 1. Each collection is the same object on every read |
| `HTMLDetailsElement`, `ToggleEvent` | `open` and `name`, and the `toggle` event that opening or closing fires in a task of the DOM manipulation task source, a `ToggleEvent` that neither bubbles nor can be canceled. Changes before that task make one event, with the old state of the first, as Blink's does. Opening one of a `name` closes the others of that name in its tree, and an open one that joins such a group, by an insertion or a new name, closes itself. A click on its main summary opens or closes it, as [Events](#events) says. A copy of an open one fires `toggle` too. `ToggleEvent` has every member |
| `HTMLDialogElement` | `open`, `returnValue`, `closedBy` with its Auto state, which is `closerequest` for a modal dialog, and `show()`, `showModal()`, `close()` and `requestClose()` with Blink's errors and events: `beforetoggle` at once, which can cancel opening, and `toggle` in a task, as `HTMLDetailsElement`'s; `close` in the next [animation frame](#animation-frames), and only in a document that has a window; and for `requestClose()` a `cancel` that can keep the dialog open, from a close watcher that exists while the dialog is open in a document with a window and closes it only if it is still that dialog's and enabled, as Blink's `CloseWatcher` does. The `open` attribute alone opens or closes it with no event. A modal dialog matches `:modal` until `close()` or its removal from the document. A dialog that `show()` or `showModal()` opens hides the popovers that it is not in, `showModal()` of a dialog that shows as a popover throws, and so does `showPopover()` of a modal one. With no layout, focus or input, it puts nothing in a top layer, makes nothing inert, moves no focus, and no Escape key or click outside closes it |
| `HTMLElement`'s popovers | `popover`, and `showPopover()`, `hidePopover()` and `togglePopover()` with Blink's errors and events: `beforetoggle` at once, which can cancel showing, and `toggle` in a task, which a later change before it runs folds into itself. Showing an `auto` or `hint` popover hides the others of the auto and hint stacks, down to the open popover that it or its `source` is in, as Blink's stacks do; a `manual` one hides none. A change of the `popover` attribute hides one that shows, with its events, and leaving the document hides it with none. A popover that shows matches `:popover-open`. With no layout, focus or input, it puts nothing in a top layer, moves no focus, has no anchor, and no Escape key, click outside or invoker button shows or hides it |
| `HTMLInputElement` | Its reflected attributes, `type`, and `checked` and `indeterminate`, with the checkedness that the `checked` attribute gives until a script or a click sets it, and a click of a checkbox or a radio button, as [Events](#events) says. A radio button group is the radio buttons of one name in one tree and one `form` ancestor, as Blink's `RadioButtonGroupScope` keeps it: checking one, by a click, `checked` or the `checked` attribute of one whose checkedness is not dirty, unchecks the rest, and so does one that is checked and joins a group, by being inserted into a form or a connected tree, or by a new name or type, so the last checked one of what the parser inserts stays checked. Blink keeps no group for radio buttons that are neither connected nor in a form, and only checking one of them unchecks the others of its tree. A copy keeps the checkedness, whether it is dirty, and `indeterminate`. A disabled `fieldset` disables nothing yet. The rest comes with forms |
| Each other element interface of the HTML Standard | Its reflected attributes. The form controls, such as `HTMLButtonElement`, get their behaviour with forms |
| `DocumentFragment` | `new DocumentFragment()`, `getElementById()` and `ParentNode`'s members. Inserting one inserts its children, as the DOM Standard says |
| `Attr`, `NamedNodeMap` | Every member. An element keeps one node for each attribute that a page asked for, which reads and writes the attribute while it is there and keeps its value once it goes, as Blink's do; `compareDocumentPosition()` puts an attribute right after its element, as Blink does |
| `DOMTokenList` | Every member over its element's content attribute, with Blink's checks and messages, `[PutForwards=value]` setters, its iterators and `toString()`. `supports()` throws for a list with no supported tokens, such as `classList` and `link.sizes`, and takes the tokens that Blink's lists support: the link types of `relList` on `link`, and `noreferrer`, `noopener` and `opener` on `a`, `area`, `form` and SVG's `a`, the sandbox tokens of `iframe.sandbox`, and `render` for the `blocking` of `link` and `style`. Each element keeps one list of each attribute. A list that a Known Gap gives, such as `script.blocking` or `output.htmlFor`, is empty and keeps nothing |
| `DOMStringMap` | `dataset` of an HTML, SVG or MathML element: a named property for each `data-` attribute, with Blink's conversions of names, checks and messages, which setting and deleting one write back to the element. Its named properties hide those of its prototype, as `[LegacyOverrideBuiltIns]` says |
| `SVGElement` and the 70 interfaces of SVG's elements that inherit from it, `MathMLElement` | Their reflected attributes, and SVG's animated strings, booleans, enumerations, integers, numbers, lengths, lists, rectangles, aspect ratios, angles, points and transforms below, with `setStdDeviation()`, `setOrientToAuto()`, `setOrientToAngle()`, `createSVGLength()`, `createSVGNumber()`, `createSVGRect()`, `createSVGAngle()`, `createSVGPoint()`, `createSVGMatrix()`, `createSVGTransform()` and `createSVGTransformFromMatrix()`, and `ownerSVGElement` and `viewportElement`, the nearest `svg`, and the nearest `svg`, `image` or `symbol`, above an element, as Blink walks up. What needs layout stays a Known Gap, `getCTM()` and `getBBox()` among it |
| `SVGAnimatedString`, `SVGAnimatedBoolean`, `SVGAnimatedEnumeration`, `SVGAnimatedInteger`, `SVGAnimatedNumber` | Every member, for each of the 103 members of SVG's elements that gives one, `className` and `href` among them: one object for each attribute of an element, whose `baseVal` reads the attribute as Blink parses it, with the attribute's initial value for one that is missing or does not parse, and keeps what a page sets, writing it to the attribute as Blink writes it, a number to six significant digits, with no record for mutation observers. `href` reads and writes `xlink:href` while only that is set. `animVal` is `baseVal`, as no animation runs. Blink writes such an attribute when something first reads it, and the Runtime at once, so one that a page's setter adds may come at another place among the element's attributes. `SVGMarkerElement`'s `orientType` is the orient of its `orientAngle`, below |
| `SVGLength`, `SVGAnimatedLength` | Every member, for each of the 65 members of SVG's elements that gives an animated length, and `createSVGLength()`. A length holds what Blink holds, a CSS value: a number in a unit, a number alone being user units, a math function or text with `var()`, which reads as 0. It reads its attribute as the property `x` in SVG's attribute mode, with the attribute's initial value for one that does not parse, and writes back the CSS text, with no record for mutation observers. A length in an absolute unit converts as Blink converts it, held to a CSS length's range; a relative one of an element in no document throws Blink's `NotSupportedError`. In a document, a percentage resolves against the nearest `svg` element's `viewBox`, or its `width` and `height`, as Blink's does for a viewport that its own attributes size, and `em` and `rem` against the initial 16 pixels. Known Gaps: Blink sizes an outermost `svg` element by its layout box, which the window stands for here, and resolves `ex`, `ch`, `lh`, the viewport units and the rest against the element's own font and the frame, which here resolve as a media query's do; a math function with a percentage does not parse yet; and `textLength` with no attribute is 0, where Blink measures the text. An `animVal` throws Blink's `NoModificationAllowedError` for a change |
| `SVGNumber`, `SVGNumberList`, `SVGAnimatedNumberList`, `SVGLengthList`, `SVGAnimatedLengthList`, `SVGStringList` | Every member, for the number lists of `values`, `tableValues`, `kernelMatrix` and `rotate`, the length lists of text's `x`, `y`, `dx` and `dy`, and `SVGTests`' `requiredExtensions` and `systemLanguage` on the elements that include it, as Blink's list tear-offs give them. An animated list's items are its attribute's, as Blink parses it: numbers in SVG's grammar, lengths each as an `SVGLength` reads one, and strings that spaces or, for `systemLanguage`, commas part. Its objects share each item, so a change through one writes the attribute, with no record for mutation observers; `getItem()` gives a new object each time. Inserting an object that is in no list puts its own item in, so a change through it reaches the list, and inserting one that a list or an animated length holds puts a copy in. A change to the attribute replaces the items, whose old objects keep their values, and an empty list removes its attribute. Indices work as `getItem()` and `replaceItem()` do, an index past the end giving `undefined`, and an `animVal`'s list and items throw Blink's `NoModificationAllowedError` for a change |
| `SVGRect`, `SVGAnimatedRect`, `SVGPreserveAspectRatio`, `SVGAnimatedPreserveAspectRatio`, `SVGAngle`, `SVGAnimatedAngle`, `SVGPoint`, `SVGPointList` | Every member, as Blink's tear-offs give them: for `viewBox` and `preserveAspectRatio` on the elements that include `SVGFitToViewBox`, `preserveAspectRatio` on `image` and `feImage` too, a marker's `orientAngle` and `orientType`, a polyline's and a polygon's `points` and `animatedPoints`, and `createSVGRect()`, `createSVGAngle()` and `createSVGPoint()`. Each reads its attribute as Blink parses it, with the initial value for one that is missing or does not parse, and writes back what a page sets with no record for mutation observers: a rectangle as its four numbers, an aspect ratio as its alignment and `meet` or `slice`, and an angle as its number and unit, or as `auto` or `auto-start-reverse` while a marker's orient is not an angle. A `turn` reads as an angle of unknown unit whose string is 0, and setting one through `valueAsString` throws Blink's `SyntaxError` after Blink's rollback, which reads the old string again and writes no attribute. Blink writes the rolled-back angle when the attribute is next read if a change through `orientAngle` or `orientType` still waits to be written, which in the Runtime none does, so after `auto` the attribute may read `0` in Chrome and stay `auto` here. `setOrientToAuto()` and `setOrientToAngle()` set the attribute as a page would. Point lists behave as the lists above. A `viewBox` with an area is what percentages of lengths inside its svg element take |
| `SVGMatrix`, `SVGTransform`, `SVGTransformList`, `SVGAnimatedTransformList` | Every member, as Blink's tear-offs give them: for the `transform` of graphics elements and `clipPath`, `gradientTransform` and `patternTransform`, `SVGPoint`'s `matrixTransform()`, and `createSVGMatrix()`, `createSVGTransform()` and `createSVGTransformFromMatrix()`. A matrix is Blink's `AffineTransform`, in doubles, with its arithmetic: each of a matrix's methods gives a new matrix, `inverse()` throws Blink's `InvalidStateError` for a matrix whose determinant is not a normal number, and `rotateFromVector()` its `InvalidAccessError` for a zero argument. A list reads its attribute as Blink parses it, any function that does not parse emptying it, and writes each transform back as its function: a translation, a scale, a rotation with the center found again from its matrix, a skew, or a matrix, each number to six significant digits. A transform's `matrix` is the same object on every read, and a change through it makes the transform a matrix. `consolidate()` multiplies the transforms into one and, as Blink commits no change of it, writes no attribute until a later change does. Lists behave as the lists above |

An element of the HTML namespace gets the HTML Standard's interface for its name, `HTMLUnknownElement` for a name the standard does not know and `HTMLElement` for a valid custom element name, as Chrome makes them. Each of those interfaces has a class, so every name gets Chrome's interface, and a name behind a runtime-enabled feature, such as `fencedframe` or `camera`, gets its interface while the Page's feature set has the feature on, and `HTMLUnknownElement` otherwise, as Blink's `HTMLElementFactory` checks. An element of the SVG namespace gets SVG's interface for its name, case-sensitively, and `SVGElement` for a name SVG does not know, and every element of the MathML namespace gets `MathMLElement`, as in Chrome. Every element and attribute has a namespace, a prefix and a local name, as Blink's `QualifiedName`: `createElementNS()` and `setAttributeNS()` check a qualified name and its namespace as Blink does, with its messages, and Blink's split of a name with two colons, which takes the local name from between them; `getAttribute()` finds an attribute with a prefix by its qualified name as given, and one without by its local name, in lower case for an HTML element of an HTML document; and markup writes each element's qualified name, as Chrome's does, where the HTML Standard writes the local name of an HTML, SVG or MathML element. Event handler attributes have Chrome's behaviour, as [What a Known Gap does](#what-a-known-gap-does) says.

`innerHTML` and `outerHTML` write the HTML Standard's serialization, escaping in attributes and text what Chrome escapes.

Known Gaps of these interfaces that need no layout, each with its route:

- `HTMLElement`'s `autocorrect`, which reads the form owner's attribute, comes with forms ([#281](https://github.com/darionxt/mitto/issues/281)).
- `Element`'s `setHTMLUnsafe()` and `setHTML()`, and the HTML-setting methods of `Element` and `CharacterData`, `afterHTML()`, `beforeHTML()`, `prependHTML()`, `appendHTML()`, `replaceWithHTML()`, their `…Unsafe` forms and their `stream…` forms, with the Sanitizer API that the safe forms need, wait on the owner's decision.
- `Document`'s `evaluate()` and `createExpression()`, XPath, which no ticket has, wait on the owner's decision.
- `Document`'s `captureEvents()`, `releaseEvents()` and `clear()` do nothing in Chrome, which their default gives.

### Selectors

`querySelector()`, `querySelectorAll()`, `matches()`, `closest()` and `webkitMatchesSelector()` parse their selectors with the selector parser in [`native/css`](../native/css), which reads the tokens of the CSS tokenizer there as Blink's `CSSSelectorParser` reads them, and match them with [`selector_checker.cc`](../native/dom/selector_checker.cc), which follows Blink's `SelectorChecker` in its mode for queries. So what parses is what Chrome takes: every pseudo-class and pseudo-element of Blink's tables whose runtime-enabled features the Page has, the forgiving lists of `:is()` and `:where()`, relative selectors in `:has()`, `:nth-child()` and `:nth-last-child()` with `of`, `&` as `:scope`, the namespace prefixes `*` and the empty one but no other, since there is no style sheet, Blink's limits on what may follow a pseudo-element, and its unknown `::-webkit-` pseudo-elements. An empty selector and one that is not valid throw Chrome's `SyntaxError`. A selector with a pseudo-element is valid and matches nothing. The fuzz target `css_selector_fuzzer` feeds the parser any input.

Names of HTML elements and their attributes match in any case in an HTML document, and so do SVG's and MathML's camel-cased ones there; IDs and classes do in quirks mode, and so do the values of the attributes that the HTML Standard lists, on HTML elements. The structural, logical and tree pseudo-classes match as in Chrome, and the pseudo-classes that read an element's state are answered as Chrome answers them for a document that no one has typed in, clicked or focused, from the element's tag name, attributes and tree, in [`pseudo_class_state.cc`](../native/dom/pseudo_class_state.cc): `:link` and `:any-link`, `:enabled` and `:disabled` with fieldsets, legends, optgroups and selects, `:checked` with the checkedness that an `HTMLInputElement` keeps and a select's default option, `:default`, `:indeterminate` with a checkbox's `indeterminate` and a radio group that has none checked, `:required` and `:optional`, `:read-only` and `:read-write` with `contenteditable` and `designMode`, `:placeholder-shown`, `:valid` and `:invalid` with a value that is missing, an email address or URL that is not one, a `pattern` that V8 compiles with the flag `v`, and a number or date out of its range or step, `:in-range` and `:out-of-range`, `:defined` from the name or `is` value an element was made with, as `createElement()`'s options, the parser and `cloneNode()` give it, `:open`, `:modal` for a dialog that `showModal()` opened, `:popover-open` for a popover that shows, `:dir()` from the first strong character for `dir=auto`, and `:lang()`. What needs input, focus, a frame or media, such as `:hover`, `:focus`, `:fullscreen`, `:autofill`, `:visited` and `:state()`, never matches, as in a page without them.

Known Gaps of selectors:

- `:target` matches the element that the fragment of the document's URL indicates, as Blink's `TreeScope::FindAnchor()` finds it: the element of that ID, or else the first `a` of that name, for the fragment as it is and then percent-decoded as UTF-8. A document of a window takes it once it has finished parsing and again after each navigation within it, and keeps it while the element moves, as Chrome keeps its CSS target. Each time, the indicated element is revealed, as Blink's `ElementFragmentAnchor` reveals it: every closed `details` element whose contents hold it opens, but not for its summary, which shows, and every ancestor whose `hidden` is `until-found` gets a `beforematch` event, which bubbles, and then loses the attribute, innermost first, until one that a listener has disconnected or changed. Chrome skips this when it restores a scroll offset from history, which a Page, without layout, never does. That the element then scrolls into view is M9's, with layout.
- `:lang()` reads the language of a document from the first language of the response's `Content-Language`, and from a `content-language` meta once it is in the document, the last that set it, which removing the meta leaves, as Blink's `Document::SetContentLanguage()` keeps it.
- `:valid` and `:invalid` take an email address with a domain that is not ASCII as not valid, where Blink converts the domain with UTS 46 first.
- A control's form is the one its `form` attribute names, or its nearest form ancestor, but not the one that the parser's form element pointer gives a control outside its form in misnested markup.
- Chrome focuses an element with `autofocus` at its first rendering, and `:focus` then matches it, where a Page focuses nothing.
- A control's selectedness and value are read from its attributes and tree at each match, where Chrome keeps them once it has them, as its form control classes do, so the two differ once a script changes those attributes. The form controls of [#281](https://github.com/darionxt/mitto/issues/281) keep that state. Checkedness is not among them: `HTMLInputElement` keeps it as Chrome does.

### Collections

`getElementsByTagName()`, `getElementsByTagNameNS()` and `getElementsByClassName()` of a document or an element, and `getElementsByName()` of a document, give live collections, the same object for the same call while a page keeps it, as Blink's `NodeListsNodeData` caches them. Each collection keeps the elements it found until a child is inserted or removed anywhere or an attribute changes, so a loop over one reads the tree once. `getElementsByTagName()` matches the qualified name, in lower case for an HTML element of an HTML document, `getElementsByClassName()` the classes split on ASCII whitespace, in any case in quirks mode, and `getElementsByName()` the HTML elements of that name. `querySelectorAll()` gives a static `NodeList`. A name of `document.all` gives the one element of that ID, or of that name among the elements that the HTML Standard names so, such as `a`, `form` and `img`, or an `HTMLCollection` of them when there are more, through its named properties, `namedItem()`, `item()` and a call.

### Ranges and traversal

`Range` and `StaticRange` follow Blink's, with its checks and messages. A `Range` is live: inserting and removing children, changing a text's data, `splitText()` and `normalize()` move its boundary points as the DOM Standard says, and `normalize()` merges and removes text as Blink's does, in post-order. `deleteContents()`, `extractContents()`, `cloneContents()`, `insertNode()` and `surroundContents()` take Blink's steps, and `createContextualFragment()` parses as `innerHTML` does for the start's element, or the body, and drops `html`, `head` and `body` but their children, in an HTML document. Its geometry, `getClientRects()` and `getBoundingClientRect()`, needs layout and stays a Known Gap until M9, and so does `expand()`, which needs it too. `TreeWalker` and `NodeIterator` walk as Blink's do, call a filter that is a function or has `acceptNode()` with Blink's errors, refuse a filter that calls them again, and a `NodeIterator` moves its reference node when the node that holds it goes, as Blink's does.

### localStorage and sessionStorage

`localStorage` and `sessionStorage` are the Session's, which keeps them for all its Pages as Chrome's storage service keeps them for a profile's tabs ([ADR-0010](adr/0010-a-directory-of-its-own-for-page-storage.md)). The Page keeps Blink's side ([`native/dom/cached_storage_area.h`](../native/dom/cached_storage_area.h)): the whole map of each area it uses, loaded on first use with one call that waits for the Session, as Blink's `GetAll()` waits, each change sent on its own, and the changes that the Session tells of, applied to the map ([The messages](#the-messages), 27 to 37). The Session ([`native/storage/storage_area_impl.h`](../native/storage/storage_area_impl.h)) keeps the map of each area and checks its quota, as Chrome's `StorageAreaImpl` does, and tells every Page that has an area of `localStorage` of each change to it, the writer's too. It tells a Page of no change to its `sessionStorage`, whose source of truth the Page is, as Chrome's `SessionStorageAreaImpl` tells a renderer of none.

- **Keys.** An area's storage key is the library's, from the URL that it loaded the document at that binds the area and from the frame tree, never the Page's word, as Chrome's browser works it out with third-party storage partitioning, which Chrome turns on: the document's origin, the site of the top-level document, and whether a document from this one up is of another site. A top-level document's key is its origin, first party, and a frame of another site, or under one, has storage apart from the same origin's at the top level. A frame under a top-level document of an opaque origin, such as a data: URL's, which Chrome keys by that origin, keeps its storage in memory for the frames under that document, for as long as the Page. A frame's about:blank or srcdoc document has its origin, its creator's, and so its key. The area stays the Page's for its other documents of that key, as Blink's cached area stays the renderer's, until the areas that no window uses hold 5 MiB, as in Blink, or number 2,048, half of the 4,096 that the Session lets a Page bind at once, where Chrome's browser takes any number. Such an area goes, and loads again from the Session when a window reaches it, which no script can tell. The documents of a Page's 1,000 frames bind fewer, and a window that would bind more, beside those of gone frames that a script still holds, has no storage, as `null`. A document of an opaque origin, such as a tab's about:blank, gets Chrome's `SecurityError`, "Access is denied for this document.", or "Storage is disabled inside 'data:' URLs." for a data: URL, and so does every member of a `Storage` whose window has gone with its frame or its document.
- **Quota.** 10 MiB for each area, counted as two bytes for each character of its keys and values. Past it, `setItem()` throws a `QuotaExceededError` with Chrome's message, "Setting the value of 'key' exceeded the quota.", whose `quota` and `requested` are `null`. The Session allows 100 KiB more, counted in the bytes that Blink sends, for writes of two Pages that race, and refuses what goes past that, which the writer's Page then takes back, as Blink's does. Neither kind counts in `navigator.storage.estimate()`, as in Chrome.
- **The order of `key()`** is the order of Blink's hash map of the keys, not of their insertion: Blink's hash of each key, its probe, its table's sizes and the buckets that a removal leaves ([`native/dom/storage_area_map.h`](../native/dom/storage_area_map.h)).
- **`storage` events.** Each window of a Page that has made its `Storage` object, as a listener of `storage` events makes both, hears of each change that another window of its storage key makes, a page's or a frame's, from a task on the DOM manipulation task source, with the writer's document's URL and its own `Storage` as `storageArea`, as Blink queues them. `localStorage`'s changes reach the Session's other Pages of the same storage key, and `sessionStorage`'s stay in their Page. A change to the same value and a `clear()` of an empty area fire none.
- **Kept where.** The Session keeps `localStorage` in its storage directory, in the SQLite database `LocalStorage` with the schema and the compression of values of Chrome's own SQLite store, or in a database in memory without one, as Chrome's Incognito mode keeps it ([From Python](#from-python)). It writes changes in Chrome's batches, a moment after they come and at most 60 times and 10 MiB an hour for each area, and all that is left when the Session closes, the changes that still wait for an area to load included, such as a `clear()` before any read, which Chrome's storage service drops when it shuts down. `sessionStorage` is the Page's own, which a Session keeps while the Page is open, and goes with the Page, which no later Session restores.

The Page program given a script has about:blank's document, so it has no storage.

### Structured clone and messaging

`structuredClone()`, `postMessage()`, `MessageChannel`, `MessagePort`, `MessageEvent` and `BroadcastChannel` follow Blink's, with Chrome's checks, errors and order of steps, and serialize with [`native/dom/serialized_script_value.cc`](../native/dom/serialized_script_value.cc), as Blink's `V8ScriptValueSerializer` writes a value: V8's own serializer inside Blink's envelope and trailer, with Blink's tags.

- **What a clone copies.** What V8 copies itself, with Chrome's messages for what it refuses, such as `() => {} could not be cloned.`; the Runtime's objects that Blink copies, `DOMRect`, `DOMRectReadOnly`, `DOMException` and `QuotaExceededError`, and `Blob`s and `File`s, as [The File API](#the-file-api) says; WebAssembly modules within the Page; and `ArrayBuffer`s, `MessagePort`s and streams that the transfer list moves, which it detaches, neuters or locks, with Blink's errors for a duplicate, a detached buffer, a WebAssembly memory's buffer, a port that was moved already, a stream that is locked or not in the list, and an object that Blink does not transfer. An object of an interface that Blink copies but the Runtime has no class for, such as `DOMPoint` or `ImageData`, comes back as an object of that interface with nothing behind it, as a Known Gap gives one. Every other object of the Runtime's throws `DataCloneError` with Chrome's message, such as `Event object could not be cloned.`, and so does one of an interface whose copy takes more than its interface, which Blink's modules write: a `FileSystemDirectoryHandle` or `FileSystemFileHandle`, which carries a transfer token and reaches only a context of the sender's origin, or an `RTCCertificate`, which carries its keys, each of which a Page has only as a Known Gap gives it, where Chrome copies it: a Known Gap, and so does a `SharedArrayBuffer` from a context that is not cross-origin isolated, such as the buffer of a shared `WebAssembly.Memory`: `SharedArrayBuffer transfer requires self.crossOriginIsolated.`. From one that is, a `SharedArrayBuffer` goes to the copy as a new object on the same memory, once for each buffer however often the value holds it, within the Page's windows, workers and their ports, a worker of a `data:` URL among them, which is of its creator's agent cluster though not isolated ([Cross-origin isolation](#cross-origin-isolation)). A `BroadcastChannel` message that holds one stays in the Page, as one that holds a WebAssembly module does, and its data is null there, as in Chrome, whose channels carry a message through the browser, which takes no shared memory along. Blink also transfers the objects of `ImageBitmap` and `OffscreenCanvas`, which have no class yet, so a transfer list that names one is refused with "Value at index 0 does not have a transferable type.": a Known Gap. An `OffscreenCanvas` outside the list throws Chrome's `An OffscreenCanvas could not be cloned because it was not transferred.`
- **`window.postMessage()`** goes to the window that it is called on, the page's or a frame's. Its sender is the incumbent window, whose script makes the call, whichever window's function it calls, as Blink's `DOMWindow::postMessage()` takes it. It serializes first and then checks its target origin, as Blink does: `*`, `/` for the sender's own, or an origin that a URL gives, else Chrome's `SyntaxError`. The message event comes from a task of its own, of Blink's posted-message type, in the recipient's window, with the sender's origin, `null` for an opaque one, the sender's window as its source, and the moved ports. The source is `null` once the sender's frame shows another window, and stays that window when the frame is removed. One of another site, whose frame Chrome has in another process, stays the source from document to document of the frame's, as Blink's `RemoteDOMWindow` does, until the frame shows a document of the recipient's site, and one whose frame was removed, or committed another document, before its message came has none ([`remote_window.h`](../native/dom/remote_window.h)). Known Gap: a document whose `Origin-Agent-Cluster: ?1` asks for a process of its origin's own counts as one of its site's. One that a window posts to itself carries the scheduler's state of the task that posted it, as Blink's task attribution does. A message for another origin than the recipient's fires nothing, and the recipient's console warns as Chrome's does, but not of one between sites, which Chrome's browser drops. A value locked to its agent cluster, with a `SharedArrayBuffer` or a WebAssembly module, fires `messageerror` in a window of another origin. `structuredClone()` runs in the realm of the window that it is called on, as Blink's does. The property `postMessage` of a window of another origin throws `no access` until [#286](https://github.com/darionxt/mitto/issues/286), but a window's own function, called on that one, sends the message. Known Gap: a callback that no script of its own calls, such as a bound `postMessage()` that a timer calls, has the window that the timer is of as its sender, where Chrome's is the one whose script set it. `includeUserActivation` gives a `UserActivation` whose members are `false`, and `delegate` fails as Chrome's fails without a user's activation.
- **`MessageChannel` and `MessagePort`** pass messages within the Page as Blink's ports pass them over mojo message pipes ([`native/dom/message_port.h`](../native/dom/message_port.h)): a port that has started reads as `mojo::Connector` does, on Blink's posted-message queue, so the first message posts a task that dispatches it alone, and each message that waits then gets a task of its own at the back of the queue. Setting `onmessage` or `onmessageerror` starts a port, a closed port drops what it has not read and its peer closes once it has read the rest, a port's `postMessage()` does nothing once it is closed or moved, a channel that schedules within the window carries the scheduler's state, and a port stays alive while it has started and is entangled. A message is checked where it comes, whichever context the port was in when it was sent: a WebAssembly module fires `messageerror` in a context of another origin, such as a worker of a `data:` URL, and a `SharedArrayBuffer` in one of another agent cluster, a window's by its origin and a worker's by its creator's, as Blink's `MessagePort::CreateMessageEvent()` has it.
- **`BroadcastChannel`** delivers as Chrome's browser process does ([`native/dom/broadcast_channel_service.h`](../native/dom/broadcast_channel_service.h)): to every other channel of its name and storage key that is open when the message reaches the service, in the order they were made, each from a task of its own on Blink's internal default queue, a hop each way as over mojo. A channel's key is its context's ([`native/dom/storage_key.h`](../native/dom/storage_key.h)), as [localStorage's](#localstorage-and-sessionstorage) is, so one of a frame under another site's document reaches only those of that partition, and one of an opaque origin only those of that origin, in its Page. The Session hands a message to each of its other Pages that has a context of that key, which the library works out from the document it loaded, not from the Page ([The messages](#the-messages), 25 and 26), so `BroadcastChannel` reaches the Session's other Pages as Chrome's reaches its other tabs, and no Page of another Session. A message carries its bytes alone, as Chrome's does through the browser, so a WebAssembly module in it reads back as `null` within the Page and gives a `messageerror` in another Page, whose agent cluster it does not reach. A message larger than a frame of the channel holds stays in its Page: a Known Gap. A Page's dedicated workers have their channels in the same service, so a worker's message reaches the window's channels and the other workers' of its origin, even when the worker closes later in the task that posted it, as one that has gone into Blink's pipe stays there. Once a worker has called `close()`, its channels post nothing and a channel it makes connects to nothing, as Blink's `BroadcastChannel` drops them, though its `postMessage()` to its `Worker` still goes.

The tests run [`tests/messaging_cases.js`](../tests/messaging_cases.js) in a Page and compare what it gives with what Chrome 157 gave for it, in `tests/data/messaging_cases.json`.

### Text encodings

`TextEncoder` and `TextDecoder` follow Blink's, with Chrome's errors, and decode with Blink's codecs, which [`native/encoding`](../native/encoding) ports: the registry of encodings and their labels, and a decoder and an encoder for UTF-8, UTF-16LE and UTF-16BE, windows-1252, x-user-defined, the CJK encodings, EUC-JP, ISO-2022-JP, Shift_JIS, EUC-KR, GBK, gb18030 and Big5, and replacement, which `TextDecoder` refuses. ICU decodes and encodes the other single-byte encodings for Blink, such as ISO-8859-2 and KOI8-R, and gives the CJK codecs their indexes, from the converters and the table of names in Chromium's ICU data, which the Page program holds whole. So a label names what it names in Chrome: each of the Encoding Standard's labels, whatever its ASCII case and the ASCII whitespace around it, and none of ICU's other names. `decode()` keeps what a stream leaves of a sequence for its next call with `stream`, drops a byte order mark of UTF-8 and UTF-16 unless `ignoreBOM`, and with `fatal` throws at the first error. It takes an `ArrayBuffer` or a view on one that is neither shared nor resizable, as Blink's `[PassAsSpan]` does. `encodeInto()` writes whole code points into a `Uint8Array` that is neither shared nor resizable. `TextDecoderStream` and `TextEncoderStream` are transform streams of the same codecs, as Blink's are: one decodes each chunk with a decoder that keeps what a chunk leaves of a sequence for the next, and refuses a chunk that is neither an `ArrayBuffer` nor a view, and the other encodes each string as UTF-8, keeping a high surrogate at the end of a chunk for the next ([Streams](#streams)). `Response`'s `text()` and `json()` decode UTF-8, as the Fetch Standard has them, as [fetch()](#fetch) says.

A document's body decodes as Blink's `TextResourceDecoder` decodes it, in [`native/encoding/text_resource_decoder.cc`](../native/encoding/text_resource_decoder.cc), with the encoding that the first of these gives:

1. **A byte order mark** of UTF-8, UTF-16LE or UTF-16BE, whatever else says.
2. **The `charset` of the response's `Content-Type`**, as `//net` parses the header for Chrome's renderer, which the Page ports since it does not link `//net`. A charset that names no encoding counts for nothing, and a `<meta>` decides then.
3. **An XML declaration** at the very start that names an encoding, even in an HTML document, unless a `<meta>` names one too. UTF-16 without a byte order mark is known by its first bytes.
4. **The prescan for a `<meta>`**, which Blink runs on the HTML tokenizer: a `<meta charset>`, or a `<meta http-equiv="Content-Type">` whose `content` names a charset, in the head, which tags inside `<title>`, `<script>` and `<style>` do not end, or in the first 1,024 bytes after it. A `<meta>` cannot name UTF-16, which is UTF-8 there, nor x-user-defined, which is windows-1252. The prescan goes on as the body comes, so a `<meta>` in a later piece decodes what follows it, and the `<title>` that came before decodes again, as in Chrome.
5. **Chrome's detector**, Compact Encoding Detection in `third_party/ced`, run as Blink's `TextEncodingDetector` runs it, on each piece until it names an encoding, with the URL's top-level domain as its hint. It never gives UTF-8 or ISO-2022-JP for a document of the web, as in Chrome, which keeps sites labelling UTF-8: a document of UTF-8 that names no encoding decodes with the default.
6. **The default**: for a frame's document of its parent's origin, the parent's encoding, which the detector also takes as its hint where the parent's detector found it, as Blink's `BuildTextResourceDecoder()` has it; or else the encoding of the URL's top-level domain in Blink's table, such as Shift_JIS for `.jp`, or else Chrome's for the language of its user interface, `intl.charset_default`, windows-1252 for the locale `en-US` of every Persona.

A document of XML or JSON that names no encoding is UTF-8, without the detector, and a style sheet's `@charset` names its encoding. `document.characterSet`, `charset` and `inputEncoding` give the encoding as the decoder has it after each piece, UTF-8 before the first. A frame takes its parent's encoding, and a frame's detector its parent's hint, when the parent is of the same origin, as Blink's `BuildTextResourceDecoder()` has it, once a Page has frames ([#285](https://github.com/darionxt/mitto/issues/285)).

A URL that a document resolves, such as a script's `src`, has its query in the document's encoding, as the URL Standard says and Blink's `Document::CompleteURL()` does it, with each character that the encoding lacks as `%26%23` its code point `%3B`, and UTF-8 for a document of UTF-16. The path and the fragment stay UTF-8, and so does a URL that `fetch()`, `Request` and `URL` parse, as in Chrome.

### Events

A node dispatches an event through the tree, as the DOM Standard says and Blink's `EventDispatcher` does ([`event_dispatcher.h`](../native/dom/event_dispatcher.h)). Its path ([`event_path.h`](../native/dom/event_path.h)) is the node, each ancestor in turn, and for a document that has a window, the window, but for an event of type `load`, which Blink never takes to the window. The capturing listeners run from the window down, then at the target its capturing listeners and then its others, each in the order they were added, then for an event that bubbles the other listeners back up to the window. `stopPropagation()`, `cancelBubble` and `stopImmediatePropagation()` stop it as in Chrome, and a dispatch clears them, sets `eventPhase` to `NONE` and `currentTarget` to null once it is done, but for the window's `load`, which Chrome leaves at the window's target phase. `composedPath()` gives the path while a listener runs, and an event of type `load` at a node has no `target` once its dispatch is done, as in Chrome. A target that is not a node, such as an `AbortSignal` or the window itself, dispatches to its own listeners alone. Each node's tree holds the event's target as its listeners see it, which is the node that dispatches until shadow trees come, which retarget it to their host there.

Listeners of `touchstart`, `touchmove`, `wheel` and `mousewheel` on a window, a document, its root element or its body are passive unless they say `passive: false`, as Blink makes them, and `preventDefault()` in a listener that says `passive: true` cancels nothing and writes Chrome's console error, "Unable to preventDefault inside passive event listener invocation.". The Runtime's own events, such as `DOMContentLoaded`, are trusted, and `dispatchEvent()` makes an event untrusted.

`document.createEvent()` makes an event of the interfaces that Blink's `EventFactory` takes ([`event_factory.h`](../native/dom/event_factory.h)), by name in any ASCII case, with the aliases `Events`, `HTMLEvents`, `SVGEvents`, `UIEvents`, `MouseEvents` and `KeyboardEvents`: an empty type, neither bubbling nor cancelable, and not initialized, so `dispatchEvent()` throws Chrome's `InvalidStateError` until `initEvent()` sets its type. Any other name throws Chrome's `NotSupportedError`, among them those that Chrome 157 no longer takes, such as `ErrorEvent` and `PopStateEvent`, and `TouchEvent` on a Machine without a touch screen. An event's `timeStamp` is the time it was made, from the window's time origin, coarsened with the clamper of `performance.now()`, so it falls between two calls of `now()` around its making. A listener object whose `handleEvent` cannot be called throws a `TypeError` that nothing reports: the window gets no `error` event and the console no error, as in Chrome, where the bindings throw it outside any script, while an exception from the getter of `handleEvent` is reported like any other.

`click()` fires Chrome's click of a script: a `PointerEvent` that bubbles, can be canceled and is composed, with the window as its `view` and `pointerId` -1, untrusted, and nothing at an element already in the middle of its own `click()`. A click that nothing cancels, `click()`'s or a page's `MouseEvent`, fires a trusted `DOMActivate` at its target, and then runs the activation behaviour of the activation target, as Blink's `CleanUpActivationBehavior` steps do. A checkbox and a radio button have theirs, as Blink's `CheckboxInputType` and `RadioInputType` have it ([`html_input_element.h`](../native/dom/html_input_element.h)): a click of a `MouseEvent` with the primary button turns a checkbox's checkedness over and clears its `indeterminate`, or checks a radio button and unchecks the others of its group, before the listeners run; a click that a listener cancels puts back the checkedness, the `indeterminate` flag and the radio button that was checked; and one that nothing cancels fires `input` and then `change` at a connected element whose checkedness changed, and no `DOMActivate`. Every other `input` element has activation behaviour that comes with forms, as submit buttons do, and an HTML link's navigates ([Navigations a page starts](#navigations-a-page-starts)). A link with `href`, SVG's `a` and a button have activation behaviour, as Blink's do, so a click inside one leaves a summary alone; a button submits nothing yet. The main summary of a `details` element, its first `summary` child, has its own, as Blink's `HTMLSummaryElement` has ([`html_details_element.h`](../native/dom/html_details_element.h)): the click opens or closes the details, unless it was on a form control, a label, an embed or an element with activation behaviour of its own inside the summary.

#### Errors at the window

An error that a script leaves uncaught fires an `ErrorEvent` at the window, as Blink's `V8Initializer` and `ExecutionContext` fire it ([`error_reporting.h`](../native/dom/error_reporting.h)): it does not bubble and can be canceled, its `message` is V8's, such as `Uncaught TypeError: x is not a function`, its `filename` the script's URL, or the document's for a script that the Session evaluated, its `lineno` and `colno` where it was thrown, counted from 1, and its `error` what was thrown. V8's message reads only an object's data properties, but for what a callback or a classic script threw that is not an error, it says what the object's `toString()` gives, run once, or `Uncaught exception` if that throws, as V8 reports what Blink's verbose `TryCatch` caught: so a `DOMException`, whose name and message are accessors, says `Uncaught TypeError: x`. What a module's evaluation throws, and what `reportError()` gets, says only what V8 reads, `Uncaught ` for a `DOMException`. An inline script's place counts from the document's start, from where the parser found the script, as Blink's `ClassicScript` has it. A script of another origin fetched without CORS shows the window's listeners `Script error.`, an empty `filename`, 0 for both places and a null `error`, as Chrome's do, though the Session still hears what it threw. The Session hears what Chrome's console says: the event's `message`, or for a `DOMException` with a message, `Uncaught ` and its name and message, as Blink's `ErrorEvent::MessageForConsole()` has it. A listener that cancels the event, or an `onerror` that returns `true`, keeps the error from the Session. An error thrown while the window's error listeners run goes to the Session alone, after the one they ran for. `reportError()` reports its argument the same way, with the place where an error was made, or else where `reportError()` was called.

A promise rejected with no handler fires a `PromiseRejectionEvent` of type `unhandledrejection` at the window, which can be canceled, from the task that reports it, which the end of the next microtask checkpoint queues, as Blink's event loop notifies about rejected promises there: so a rejection in one callback of an animation frame or one listener is reported before what the next callback queues, and only what no listener canceled reaches the Session. A handler that comes after that report fires `rejectionhandled` from a later task. The rejection of a script whose errors are muted fires neither, as in Chrome. The Session's scripts are compiled as DevTools compiles what it evaluates, so their errors are not muted.

#### Mutation observers

`MutationObserver` and `MutationRecord` follow Blink's ([`mutation_observer.h`](../native/dom/mutation_observer.h)). Each node holds the registrations of the observers that observe it, and each change to a node's children, attributes or data queues a record for each observer that a registration on the node, or for a subtree on an ancestor, gives an interest in it: an observer that asked for old values gets the record, and the others share one with a null `oldValue`. The changes that one operation makes to a node's children are one record while each continues where the last left off ([`child_list_mutation_scope.h`](../native/dom/child_list_mutation_scope.h)), so `replaceChildren()`, `textContent` and `innerHTML` make one each, and a node that moves first leaves its parent with a record of its own. The record of an insertion is queued before an inserted script runs, as Blink's scope ends before its post-insertion steps, but for `replaceChildren()` and `textContent`, whose record waits for them, as in Blink. The parser's changes make records too. The observers that have records get them in a microtask, which the first record of a delivery queues, in the order the observers were made, with the records and the observer, and the observer as `this`. A subtree observer of a node that leaves the tree keeps hearing of changes under it until its next delivery. `observe()` checks its options with Blink's messages, and `takeRecords()` and `disconnect()` work as in Chrome.

### Abort signals

`AbortController` and `AbortSignal` follow Blink's, in [`native/dom`](../native/dom). `abort()` aborts the signal with its reason, or with an `AbortError` that says "signal is aborted without reason", sets the reason of every signal that follows it, then runs the abort algorithms of the signal, in the order they were added, and fires `abort`, then does the same for each signal that follows it, as Blink's `AbortSignal::SignalAbort()` does. `AbortSignal.abort()` makes an aborted signal, and `AbortSignal.timeout()` one that a timer of the Page's event loop aborts with a `TimeoutError`, queued as `setTimeout()` queues its timers, whose timer stops when the window's document goes, as Blink cancels its task. `AbortSignal.any()` makes a signal that follows the sources of the signals it is given: those that are not themselves made by `any()`, and the sources of those that are, as Blink's composition managers link them. A source holds the signals that follow it weakly, so one that nothing reaches goes, and holds strongly one that has `abort` listeners, which Blink's `AbortSignalRegistry` keeps instead. `throwIfAborted()` throws the reason as it is, with no context in front of its message, as Blink's `OmitExceptionContextInformation` leaves it.

`addEventListener()`'s `signal` adds nothing once the signal has aborted, and otherwise adds an abort algorithm that removes the listener of that type, function and capture, as Blink's does, whichever registration that is by then, which the listener keeps while the target keeps it. The algorithm finds the function by a number that it keeps on it in a private property, which a page cannot see, so it keeps no function alive. `TaskController` gives a `TaskSignal` of the priority it is made with. Changing the priority is a Known Gap, since nothing in the Runtime runs tasks by priority yet. What a signal does to `fetch()` is in [fetch()](#fetch).

### Streams

`ReadableStream`, `WritableStream` and `TransformStream` follow Blink's, which [`native/dom`](../native/dom) ports from Blink's `core/streams` file by file: each class with its controllers and readers, the default controller and the byte stream controller, default readers and BYOB readers with `min`, BYOB requests and `autoAllocateChunkSize`, `ByteLengthQueuingStrategy` and `CountQueuingStrategy`, whose `size` functions each window makes once, `tee()` of a default stream and of a byte stream, `pipeTo()` and `pipeThrough()` with their options and signal, and `for await`, `values()` and `preventCancel` over a readable stream. Their algorithms run in the order of Blink's, with promises of the Page's context driving each step, so what a page logs of reads, writes and its own microtasks comes in Chrome's order, and they throw Chrome's errors with Chrome's messages, including those that Blink's own code gives where the Streams Standard says less, such as "Invalid type is specified". As in Blink, a source's `cancel()`, of a default stream or of a byte stream, gets the controller as a second argument. `CompressionStream` and `DecompressionStream` run Chromium's zlib for `gzip`, `deflate` and `deflate-raw`, the formats of Chrome 157, with Blink's buffers and errors. zlib writes in a gzip header the operating system that it was built for, so a Page's header says Windows, 10, on every Host, as Windows Chrome's does, where Mac Chrome's says 19. A readable stream's async iterator is an object of the iterator interface that the generator writes, with the stream's iteration source behind it ([The bindings](#the-bindings)), and each result of its `next()` and `return()` has `done` before `value`, as Blink's `ESCreateIterResultObject()` makes it, where a read's result has them in the same order as Blink's dictionary.

A Known Gap that gives a readable stream, such as WebTransport's streams, gives a closed, empty `ReadableStream`, and one that gives a writable stream, such as `SerialPort`'s `writable` or `FileSystemFileHandle`'s `createWritable()`, a `WritableStream` whose sink takes each chunk and keeps nothing, each of the interface the IDL names ([What a Known Gap does](#what-a-known-gap-does)).

The `body` of a `Response` or a `Request` is a byte stream of Blink's `BodyStreamBuffer`, `textStream()` pipes a body through a `TextDecoderStream` for UTF-8, and a stream as a `fetch()` body goes as the Session reads it, over HTTP/2 and HTTP/3 ([fetch()](#fetch)).

A `ReadableStream`, `WritableStream` or `TransformStream` in a transfer list, of `structuredClone()`, a `MessagePort`'s `postMessage()` or the window's, moves as Blink's does ([`transferable_streams.h`](../native/dom/transferable_streams.h)): over a new pair of ports, one end of which the clone carries, the original pipes into a cross-realm writable stream, or a cross-realm readable stream pipes into it, so it stays locked, and its chunks go as messages, each a copy, with backpressure of one chunk. A chunk that cannot be cloned errors both sides with Chrome's `DataCloneError`.

### The File API

`Blob`, `File`, `FileReader`, `FileReaderSync` and `ProgressEvent` follow Blink's, which [`native/dom`](../native/dom) ports from Blink's `core/fileapi`:

- **A blob's bytes.** Chrome keeps a blob's bytes in the browser, and a renderer holds a handle with the blob's UUID. A Page has no browser, so its handle holds the bytes ([`blob_data.h`](../native/dom/blob_data.h)), which never change and which every `Blob` made from them, every slice, clone and worker that has them shares, on any thread. `new Blob()` takes a string as UTF-8, with each line break written as CRLF for `endings: "native"`, as Windows Chrome writes it, a buffer or a view as its bytes, and a blob as its bytes. Its type is kept in lowercase, and dropped if it has a character outside printable ASCII. `slice()` rounds its offsets half to even, as Blink converts a `[Clamp] long long`, and clamps them to the blob as Blink's `ClampSliceOffsets()` does.
- **Reads.** `text()`, `arrayBuffer()`, `bytes()` and a `FileReader`'s reads go through Blink's `FileReaderLoader` ([`file_reader_loader.h`](../native/dom/file_reader_loader.h)). Chrome's renderer hears a blob's size from the browser in a task of the file reading task source, and its bytes in later tasks, as much as a data pipe of at most 2 MiB holds each time. A Page posts the same tasks with the bytes it holds, so a read settles after a `setTimeout(0)` set beside it, as in Chrome. `text()` decodes UTF-8, and `readAsText()` the encoding that its label names, or else UTF-8, whatever the blob's type says; in both a byte order mark wins, as Blink's decoder lets it. `readAsDataURL()` writes the blob's type, or `application/octet-stream` for none, and its bytes in base64. A `FileReader` fires `loadstart`, then `progress` at most each 50 ms and once more before `load` if any byte came, then `load` or `error`, and `loadend`, each a trusted `ProgressEvent` with the bytes read of the blob's size. `abort()` stops the read at once and fires `abort` and `loadend`, and a second read while one runs throws Chrome's `InvalidStateError`. `stream()` is a byte stream over Blink's `BlobBytesConsumer` ([`blob_bytes_consumer.h`](../native/dom/blob_bytes_consumer.h)), whose bytes come in a later networking task, a pipe's worth at a time, and `textStream()` pipes it through a `TextDecoderStream` for UTF-8. A dedicated worker's `FileReaderSync` reads at once.
- **Files.** `new File()` adds a name and a time of last modification, `lastModified` or the time it was made, which `lastModifiedDate` gives as a `Date`. A slice of a `File` is a `Blob`.
- **Clones.** A `Blob` or a `File` in `structuredClone()`, a `MessagePort`'s or the window's `postMessage()` or a `BroadcastChannel` message within the Page is written as Blink writes it, by the UUID of its bytes, with its type and size, and a file's name and time. The value carries the handle of those bytes beside its own, as Blink's carries its blobs' handles, so the copy shares the bytes.
- **Bodies.** A `Blob` as the body of a `Request`, a `Response` or a `fetch()` gives its bytes and its type, as [fetch()](#fetch) says.
- **Loading `data:` and `blob:` URLs.** A `fetch()` of either goes nowhere near the Session, as Chrome answers it without its network service ([`local_scheme_fetch.h`](../native/dom/local_scheme_fetch.h)), and adds no entry to the Performance Timeline. A `data:` URL gives, in any mode and for any method, a `basic` response of status 200 and `OK`, its type as the `Content-Type` and its bytes, none for `HEAD`, from a task of its own, as Blink's `ResourceLoader` answers it in the renderer; one that does not parse fails, and `Range` changes nothing. A `blob:` URL names the blob that it named when the `Request` was made, as Blink resolves it then, so a fetch works after the URL is revoked, and gives what the browser's `BlobURLLoader` gives, after a hop there and back, so a `setTimeout(0)` set beside it runs first: `GET` only, `Content-Length` and `Content-Type`, the blob's type or empty, a single `Range` of the Fetch Standard's grammar as a 206 with `Content-Range`, and a failure for any other `Range`, any other method, a URL that names nothing, or, in mode `no-cors`, one of another origin, as the browser's partition check fails another storage key's. One of another origin in mode `same-origin` or `cors` fails at once, as Blink's main fetch fails it, so an abort right after `fetch()` changes nothing. Its `Content-Type` is the blob's type without the spaces around it, as a header's value has none. The blob's bytes go a piece at a time as the client takes them, at most a data pipe's worth ahead, as the browser's `MojoBlobReader` writes them. `Response`'s `url` leaves out the fragment, which the response's URL keeps, so a module of a `blob:` URL with one has it in `import.meta.url`, as in Chrome. An abort of a `fetch()` of either with a stream as its body cancels the stream with the signal's reason, as the Fetch Standard's abort steps cancel a request's body. `URL.revokeObjectURL()` lets the resources of the URL go from those a later request may take, in every document of the Page, as Blink's drops it from the memory cache that a renderer's documents share, so another element's load of it fails, while a load that has started goes on, and a link's preload of it still serves a `fetch()`, as Blink keeps preloads apart. A window's `fetch()` of a `data:` or `blob:` URL takes such a preload first, as it does for any URL. A detached frame's `fetch()`, which a script kept, rejects with Chrome's `TypeError` that the global scope is shutting down, whatever the URL. A module worker's top-level graph reads its creator's `blob:` URLs, as its creator's outside settings fetch it, even for a worker of an opaque origin. A document of an opaque origin keeps it across a same-document navigation, and so the `blob:` URLs that it made. A worker's script, `importScripts()`, module imports in windows and workers and a document's own loads, such as a classic script, take a `blob:` URL the same way.
- **Blob URLs.** `URL.createObjectURL()` gives a `blob:` URL of the context's origin, `null` for an opaque one, and a new UUID, and registers it for the blob in the Page's registry ([`blob_url_registry.h`](../native/dom/blob_url_registry.h)), as Blink's `PublicURLManager` registers one with the browser's `BlobURLStore`, which any context of the Page, a frame's or a worker's, may read. `revokeObjectURL()` revokes a URL that a context of the same origin made, unless it has a fragment, and the URLs of a document or a worker go with it, as the browser revokes them. A `MediaSource`'s URL names nothing, since playing media stays out.

Known Gaps of the File API: a `blob:` URL of another Page, which Chrome's browser keeps for every renderer of its storage key; one that a frame of the same origin under a frame of another site reads, which Chrome partitions by storage key, where the Page goes by origin until frames have storage keys of their own, as for `localStorage`; a `Blob` in a value that leaves the Page, in a `BroadcastChannel` message to another Page or in IndexedDB, whose bytes Chrome's browser keeps for the other side, reads back as empty, of its type; `FileList` and the files of an `input` or of a drop, which come with forms ([#281](https://github.com/darionxt/mitto/issues/281)) and input (M9); and Blink's limit of 100 reads of a context at once, which queues the rest, where a Page runs them all.

### Media queries

`matchMedia()` parses its media query list with the CSS tokenizer and the media query parser in [`native/css`](../native/css), which follow CSS Syntax as Blink's `CSSTokenizer` runs it and Blink's `MediaQueryParser`, and which M9's CSS parser takes over. The fuzz target `media_query_fuzzer` feeds them any input. The `MediaQueryList` it gives writes the list back in `media` as Chrome writes it: lower case, Chrome's spacing, numbers with at most six significant digits, math functions simplified, `not all` for a query that does not parse, and a condition of a syntax that Blink does not know, Media Queries' `<general-enclosed>`, as it was given. Which media features parse follows the Page's runtime-enabled features, as in Blink: in the stable set of Chrome 157, `inverted-colors`, `prefers-reduced-data`, `video-dynamic-range`, `display-state` and `resizable` are experimental, so they do not parse, and a query of them is unknown and never matches, whatever the machine.

`matches` is worked out on each read, as Blink's `MediaQueryEvaluator` works it out for a tab of Windows Chrome, from what the Page's start message carries of the Session's Machine and Persona and nothing else:

| Media features | From |
| --- | --- |
| `width`, `height`, `aspect-ratio`, `orientation`, and the units `vw`, `vh`, `vmin` and the rest | The viewport |
| `device-width`, `device-height`, `device-aspect-ratio` | The display, over the scale, as `screen.width` and `screen.height` are |
| `resolution`, `-webkit-device-pixel-ratio` | The scale |
| `prefers-color-scheme`, `prefers-reduced-motion`, `prefers-reduced-transparency` | The three settings |
| `forced-colors`, `prefers-contrast` | The contrast theme: `active` and `more` with any theme a Machine can have, `none` and `no-preference` without one |
| `any-pointer` | `fine`, and `coarse` too with a touch screen. `pointer` is always `fine`, and `hover` and `any-hover` always `hover`: a desktop always has a mouse, which Windows reports even for a touchpad |
| `color`, `color-gamut`, `dynamic-range`, `monochrome`, `color-index` | 8 bits a channel, `srgb`, `standard`, 0 and 0: a Machine holds no wide-gamut or HDR display |
| `display-mode`, `scripting`, `update`, `overflow-block`, `overflow-inline`, `grid`, the viewport segments, `device-posture`, `scan` and the media types | What every tab of Windows Chrome has: `browser`, `enabled`, `fast`, `scroll`, a bitmap display, one segment, `continuous`, no `tv`, and `screen` |

A number is held between the largest floats either way when it is read, as Blink holds it, but a value that a unit or arithmetic takes past them is written as it is held. So, as in Chrome 157, `(width: calc(1e38in))` is written back as `(width: calc(9.6e+39px))`, which reads back as `(width: calc(3.40282e+38px))`.

Blink's parser recurses once for each parenthesis of a condition, as this one does, and Chrome 157 takes some 20,000 of them. So the Page program has the stack of Chrome's renderers on its main thread, 8 MiB, which on Windows its link sets as `chrome.exe`'s does, where Windows would give it 1 MiB.

A Machine does not change while a Page runs, so no `change` event is ever fired, as none would be in Chrome on that machine. `addListener()`, `removeListener()` and `addEventListener()` keep their listeners as any event target does, and a page can make its own `MediaQueryListEvent`. Blink keeps a list with change listeners alive while its document is, which only a `WeakRef` could tell apart.

Known Gaps of media queries:

- The math functions other than `calc()`, `min()`, `max()` and `clamp()`, such as `round()` or `sin()`, and a product or quotient of two dimensions, which Blink's typed arithmetic takes. A value with one does not parse, so its media feature is unknown, where Chrome computes it: `(width: round(10px, 3px))` is written back as given and never matches, where Chrome writes `(width: calc(9px))`.
- The units of the root element's font beyond `em` and `rem`: `ex`, `ch`, `ic`, `cap` and `lh` and their root forms take Blink's sizes for a document with no font data until the Runtime has fonts (roadmap, M9).
- `(fallback: none)`, a feature of container queries that Blink parses in a media query too and whose evaluation then stops Chrome's renderer, is unknown.

`media_query_test` runs the cases of Blink's `media_query_set_test.cc` and `media_query_evaluator_test.cc` that need no layout ([building.md](building.md#the-media-query-tests)), and `tests/test_page_media.py` checks what Chrome 157 writes back for a set of queries and that a Page answers each feature from its Machine.

### The tree builder

The tree builder in [`native/dom/html_tree_builder.cc`](../native/dom/html_tree_builder.cc) takes the tokens of the HTML tokenizer in [`native/html`](../native/html) and builds a document's tree, or a fragment's for `innerHTML` and `outerHTML`, with the HTML Standard's tree construction, as Blink's `HTMLTreeBuilder` and `HTMLConstructionSite` have it where Blink differs, which the code says at each place: every insertion mode, foster parenting in tables, the list of active formatting elements with the adoption agency algorithm, framesets, and SVG and MathML elements, with the case of their names and attributes and their integration points. A fragment's context can be any element, of any namespace. As in the Engine Release:

- `select` has no insertion mode of its own, as the HTML Standard has had it since customizable `select`: elements such as `b`, `svg` and `textarea` go inside it, a `select` inside another closes it, and so does an `input`, since Blink's feature `InputInSelect` is not on for stable.
- An element deeper than 512 elements goes in beside its parent instead, as in Blink.
- A start tag such as `div` or `p` in SVG or MathML pops back to HTML content in a fragment too.
- The parser's scripting flag is on for a document that has a window, whose scripts a Page always runs, so its `noscript` holds raw text. It is off, as Blink's `HTMLParserOptions` has it, for a document of no window, such as one that `createHTMLDocument()` made, and for a template's `innerHTML`, whose fragment belongs to the template's contents: `noscript`'s contents are then markup, with the "in head noscript" insertion mode, where Blink's `HTMLParserIgnoreHeadInNoscript`, on for stable, ignores a `head` start tag. The markup that a document of no window writes back escapes `noscript`'s text, as Blink's `MarkupFormatter` does, and an element with no end tag, such as `br`, writes back no children, so its `innerHTML` is empty.
- The parsing of a document's markup waits at the end of each script element, SVG's too, until the script has run ([Loading a document](#loading-a-document)), and a self-closing SVG script acts as its end tag. The builder marks each script element it makes, HTML's or SVG's, as the parser's, and as already started in a fragment, so that `innerHTML`'s scripts never run, though `createContextualFragment()`'s run once inserted, as Blink's do. A fragment's script that its markup leaves unclosed is marked already started at the end, as the HTML Standard says and Blink does, so it never runs, where a document's still runs, as in Chrome. The builder gives each script element the line and column where its text starts, as Blink's parser does.

A template's contents go in its `DocumentFragment`, and their nodes belong to the template document, as in Blink, and `xlink:href`, `xml:lang`, `xmlns` and the other foreign attributes get their namespaces. A processing instruction, such as `<?start name=a>`, makes a `ProcessingInstruction` node, as Blink's `HTMLProcessingInstruction`, on for stable, has it. A `<template for=a>` patches the document, as Blink's `DocumentPatching`, on for stable, does: the builder looks in the nearest scope that is no template, the document's element for the body, for a processing instruction named `a`. Before a `<?marker name=a>` it puts the template's children. For a `<?start name=a>`, it takes out what lies between it and its `<?end>` at once, as a page's removal does, and puts the children before the end. The markers and the template go when the template ends. A template whose name finds no marker is an ordinary one, and so is one with an empty name, which Blink's `DeclarativeFragment` would take, but that is experimental. What the DOM does not have yet goes in as near Chrome's tree as it can, and the builder records the rule it could not follow: a template with `shadowrootmode` goes in as one, as Blink puts a template whose root cannot attach, until shadow trees attach it (#276). It does not patch, as one whose root attached would not. html5lib's tree construction tests read that record to tell a rule the builder could not follow from a wrong tree ([building.md](building.md#the-tree-builders-tests)). #276 drops the record once declarative shadow roots attach, as no other rule is skipped.

### The XML parsers

An XML document, of a navigation's XML, XHTML or SVG response, of `DOMParser`'s XML types or of a document's content, is read by one of Blink's two XML parsers, which [`native/dom/document_parser.h`](../native/dom/document_parser.h) picks as Blink's `Document::CreateParser()` does: the Rust xml crate's reader, as Blink's `XMLDocumentParserRs` drives it ([`xml_tree_builder.h`](../native/dom/xml_tree_builder.h)), with `XMLParsingRust`, or with `XMLRustForNonXslt` for a document of no window or an SVG document, and libxml2, as Blink's `XMLDocumentParser` drives it ([`libxml_tree_builder.h`](../native/dom/libxml_tree_builder.h)), for any other. `XMLRustForNonXslt` is on for stable in 157, but every Persona's variations seed turns it off, so a Session's Pages read all their XML on libxml2, as Windows Chrome does, and only a Page that the program starts with a script uses the Rust reader. Both build the tree alike ([`xml_dom_builder.h`](../native/dom/xml_dom_builder.h)):

- Each element gets its namespace declarations as attributes first and then its other attributes, each name checked as Blink's `Element::ParseAttributeName()` checks it; an element whose prefix names no namespace has the prefix in its local name. A template's children go into its contents.
- Text waits until the next node, as Blink's leaf text node does; a CDATA section, a comment, a processing instruction and the doctype become their nodes; an XML declaration sets `xmlVersion`, `xmlEncoding` and `xmlStandalone`, and `XMLSerializer` writes it again for the document. The XHTML DTDs that the HTML Standard names give the document HTML's named character references.
- A script runs at its end tag in a document of a window, a deferred or external one before the parser reads on, as Blink's `XMLParserScriptRunner` runs them. A module script that would wait for the end of parsing is fetched but never runs, with Blink's console error, as that runner drops it, while an async one runs as soon as it is ready. In a document of no window, such as `DOMParser`'s, and in a fragment, scripts never run.
- Errors are each reader's own, by line and column: the Rust reader stops at its first, libxml2 reads on as far as it can and reports up to 25. The parse ends with Blink's block of errors, a `parsererror` element of the HTML namespace first in the document's element, or in an HTML body that takes the place of an SVG root or of none, and the text that waited is dropped. A lone surrogate is an error on libxml2, which reads UTF-16, and U+FFFD on the Rust reader. A byte of a navigation's body that its encoding cannot decode stops the decoder, as Blink's stops for XML, and libxml2 then reports a fatal `Encoding error` where it has got to, once a piece of text before the byte has reached it, as Blink's `XMLDocumentParser` does, while the Rust reader reads on to the end of what was decoded.
- Neither loads an external entity or DTD, as `XMLNoExternalEntities` has it, and libxml2 opens no file: its input callbacks give nothing.

`innerHTML`, `outerHTML`, `insertAdjacentHTML()` and `createContextualFragment()` in a document that is not HTML parse XML with libxml2's fragment parser, as Blink does, with the namespaces in scope on the context element, and throw Blink's `SyntaxError` for markup that is not well formed. A top-level XML document that showed no error and has no element of the HTML, SVG or MathML namespace and no style sheet's processing instruction becomes Blink's XML tree viewer ([`xml_tree_viewer.h`](../native/dom/xml_tree_viewer.h)): its nodes go into a hidden container under an HTML document with Blink's style sheet, and at the window's load a tree of spans shows them. Folding a part of that tree, which a mouse press does in Chrome, is a Known Gap, and so are a style sheet's processing instruction, which loads no style sheet until M9 brings CSS, and XSLT's, which transforms nothing, and `XSLTProcessor`, which come with [#400](https://github.com/darionxt/mitto/issues/400). The fuzz target `xml_parser_fuzzer` feeds any XML to both readers, whole and in pieces, and to libxml2's fragment parser ([building.md](building.md#sanitizer-builds-and-the-fuzz-target)).

### What the tests compare

The cases of [the bindings' tests](#what-the-tests-compare-with-chrome) hold 37 of what the DOM's interfaces do, each on elements and documents that the case makes, never on the page's own document, which is another in Chrome's page: trees that the parser builds from markup in an element, through `innerHTML`, the markup it writes back, selectors that match and selectors that are not valid, attributes, each change to a tree and the errors of those that cannot be, and the window. Chrome 157.0.8103.39 gave the same for each.

`tests/test_page_queries.py` runs [`tests/query_cases.js`](../tests/query_cases.js) in the Page program on the page that Chrome 157.0.8103.51 ran it on, and compares each part with Chrome's record: which of 219 selectors parse and the error of each that does not, what 96 selectors match in a fixture of forms, links, directions, languages and SVG, the collections and `document.all`'s names, and what `Range`, `StaticRange`, `TreeWalker` and `NodeIterator` do and throw.

## The probe

The probe records a page's **Script Fingerprint**: everything that the page's scripts can reach, and what the Persona and the Machine decide of it.
